CL-SD-009 — Telehealth & Remote Monitoring Services

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy establishes the standards for the delivery, documentation, and oversight of telehealth services and remote patient monitoring (RPM) at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. Telehealth and RPM technologies represent supplemental tools that can enhance patient monitoring, improve clinical outcomes, support earlier intervention, and reduce unnecessary hospitalizations — but they do not replace in-person skilled visits. Under current CMS rules, telehealth encounters are not counted as billable home health visits for Medicare payment purposes (though this may evolve with future CMS rulemaking). This policy ensures that telehealth services are used appropriately to complement the plan of care, are properly documented, and do not substitute for required in-person visits.

3. Scope

This policy applies to all clinical staff using telehealth or RPM technology with patients, the Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager, IT staff supporting telehealth platforms, and Operations staff managing telehealth scheduling.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 Telehealth services at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. include video visits, telephone encounters, and remote patient monitoring (e.g., automated transmission of vital signs, weight, blood glucose, oxygen saturation) used as supplements to the physician-approved plan of care.

4.2 Telehealth encounters shall not be used as a substitute for required in-person skilled visits. The agency shall ensure that all visits counted for Medicare billing purposes are in-person visits conducted in the patient's home. Telehealth encounters may supplement — but not replace — the visit frequencies specified in the plan of care.

4.3 All telehealth services shall be authorized by a physician order, reflected in the plan of care, and documented in the patient's clinical record. The plan of care shall specify the type of telehealth service, frequency, clinical parameters to be monitored, and the responsible clinician.

4.4 Patient consent for telehealth services shall be obtained and documented before initiating telehealth. The consent shall address: (a) the nature of telehealth services; (b) how the patient's data will be transmitted, stored, and used; (c) the patient's right to refuse telehealth and receive in-person services instead; (d) that telehealth does not replace in-person emergency services and the patient should call 911 in an emergency.

4.5 All telehealth technology used by the agency shall comply with HIPAA security requirements per CO-HP-002. Only agency-approved, encrypted platforms shall be used for video visits or data transmission. Consumer-grade, non-encrypted platforms (e.g., standard SMS, non-HIPAA-compliant video apps) shall not be used for clinical communications involving PHI.

4.6 RPM data shall be reviewed by the assigned clinician at minimum daily for actively monitored patients, or per the frequency specified in the plan of care. Clinically significant data (vital signs outside defined parameters, weight changes indicating fluid retention, blood glucose outside target range) shall trigger a same-day clinical response including patient contact, physician notification, and visit scheduling as clinically indicated.

4.7 The Director of Nursing shall ensure that all clinicians using telehealth are trained on the technology, documentation requirements, and clinical protocols specific to telehealth encounters.

4.8 Telehealth and RPM services shall be integrated into the agency's QAPI program per QA-PG-001, with outcome metrics tracked including: patient engagement, alert response times, hospitalization rates for RPM patients, and patient satisfaction.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Patient Enrollment in Telehealth / RPM

6.2 Telehealth Visit Execution

6.3 RPM Data Review and Response

6.4 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

CL-SD-010 — IV Therapy & Infusion Services

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy defines the clinical standards, competency requirements, safety protocols, and documentation expectations for intravenous (IV) therapy and infusion services provided by Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. IV therapy in the home setting includes peripheral and central venous access device management, IV antibiotic therapy, IV hydration, total parenteral nutrition (TPN), blood product administration (if within scope and authorized by the agency), chemotherapy infusion support (limited scope), and IV medication administration. These are among the highest-risk skilled services provided in home health due to the potential for serious complications including infection (central line-associated bloodstream infections — CLABSI), air embolism, infiltration/extravasation, adverse drug reactions, fluid overload, and anaphylaxis. This policy ensures that all IV therapy services are delivered by competency-validated RNs with the clinical judgment and emergency preparedness to manage complications in the home setting.

3. Scope

This policy applies to all RNs providing IV therapy and infusion services, the Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager, Clinical Coordinators scheduling IV visits, pharmacy partners providing IV medications and supplies, and Revenue Cycle staff verifying IV service documentation. LVNs shall not independently administer IV therapy at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. except under direct, on-site RN supervision and only for those IV tasks within the LVN's California BPC § 2859 scope.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 IV therapy services shall be authorized by a physician order that specifies: (a) the IV medication or solution; (b) dose, rate, and route (peripheral vs. central); (c) frequency and duration of infusions; (d) vascular access device type; (e) monitoring parameters; (f) laboratory testing schedule if applicable.

4.2 Only RNs who have completed the agency's IV Therapy Competency Program and demonstrated competency in all required IV skills shall be authorized to provide IV therapy services independently. The Director of Nursing shall maintain a current roster of IV-competency-validated RNs.

4.3 The IV Therapy Competency Program shall include demonstrated competency in: (a) peripheral IV insertion and management; (b) central venous access device (CVAD) management including PICC lines, Port-a-Cath access, and tunneled catheters; (c) IV medication preparation and administration; (d) infusion pump operation and troubleshooting; (e) aseptic technique and central line bundle compliance; (f) recognition and management of IV therapy complications (infiltration, extravasation, phlebitis, air embolism, catheter-related bloodstream infection, anaphylaxis); (g) emergency response for IV-related adverse events in the home setting.

4.4 Before each IV infusion, the administering RN shall: (a) verify the physician order; (b) verify patient identity using two identifiers per OP-PA-002; (c) verify the medication using the "Five Rights" (right patient, right medication, right dose, right route, right time); (d) assess the vascular access device site for signs of infection, displacement, or malfunction; (e) verify IV solution integrity (expiration date, clarity, absence of particles); (f) confirm availability of emergency supplies including anaphylaxis kit.

4.5 The RN shall remain with the patient during the initial 15 minutes of any new IV medication infusion to monitor for adverse reactions, and shall remain for the duration of any high-risk infusion (first-dose antibiotics, blood products, chemotherapy agents).

4.6 All central line care shall follow the agency's Central Line Bundle Protocol (Appendix A), which includes: hand hygiene before access; chlorhexidine skin antisepsis; sterile barrier precautions during dressing changes; optimal catheter site care; and daily assessment of line necessity.

4.7 All IV therapy complications and adverse events shall be reported to the physician immediately, documented in the clinical record, and reported through the agency's incident reporting system per RM-ER-002.

4.8 The agency shall coordinate with the infusion pharmacy for medication procurement, compounding, delivery, and clinical support. The agency shall verify that the pharmacy holds appropriate state and federal licenses.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Pre-Infusion Assessment and Preparation

6.2 IV Administration

6.3 Vascular Access Device Management

6.4 Emergency Response for IV Complications

6.5 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All RNs assigned to provide IV therapy shall complete the agency's IV Therapy Competency Program before independently performing IV services. The program shall include didactic content, skills lab/simulation, and proctored clinical competency evaluation.

10.2 IV competency shall be re-evaluated annually.

10.3 All personnel within scope shall acknowledge this policy within 14 calendar days.

CL-SD-011 — Wound Care Assessment & Management

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy defines the assessment, classification, treatment, documentation, and outcome monitoring standards for wound care services at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. Wound care is among the most frequently provided skilled nursing services in home health, encompassing a wide range of wound types including pressure injuries (pressure ulcers), surgical wounds, diabetic foot ulcers, venous stasis ulcers, arterial ulcers, traumatic wounds, skin tears, and ostomy site management. Wound care documentation is heavily scrutinized during CMS surveys, ADR audits, and OASIS validation reviews because wound status directly affects OASIS scoring (particularly M1311–M1350), PDGM clinical grouping and payment, and quality measure reporting. Inaccurate wound assessment, inconsistent wound staging, or incomplete documentation can result in: PDGM payment errors, OASIS accuracy deficiencies, quality measure underperformance, survey citations under 42 CFR § 484.60 and § 484.75, and patient harm from inadequate treatment. This policy ensures that all wound care is evidence-based, accurately documented, and aligned with CMS OASIS guidance.

3. Scope

This policy applies to all RNs and LVNs providing wound care services, the Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager, all clinicians completing OASIS wound items, and Revenue Cycle staff relying on wound documentation for coding and billing.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 All wound care services shall be authorized by a physician order specifying: the wound location, wound care procedure, dressing type and frequency, and any wound-related medications (e.g., topical antibiotics, enzymatic debriding agents).

4.2 At SOC and at each OASIS assessment time point, all wounds shall be assessed, classified, staged (for pressure injuries), measured, and documented using the standardized Wound Assessment Documentation Template (Appendix A) that includes all elements necessary for accurate OASIS item completion (M1311 through M1350).

4.3 Wound assessment shall include at minimum: (a) wound location (anatomical site); (b) wound etiology (pressure injury, surgical, diabetic, venous, arterial, traumatic, other); (c) wound stage/classification — using the National Pressure Injury Advisory Panel (NPIAP) staging system for pressure injuries, and the appropriate classification system for other wound types; (d) wound measurements — length x width x depth in centimeters; (e) wound bed description — percentage of granulation, epithelialization, slough, eschar, necrosis; (f) wound edge and periwound skin condition; (g) drainage — type (serous, sanguineous, serosanguineous, purulent), amount (scant, small, moderate, large); (h) signs of infection — erythema, warmth, edema, odor, increased drainage, dehiscence; (i) pain at the wound site; (j) current treatment being applied.

4.4 Wound photographs shall be obtained at SOC, at each recertification, and whenever a significant change in wound status occurs — with patient consent and in compliance with HIPAA per CO-HP-001. Photographs shall be taken with consistent technique (ruler for scale, consistent lighting, perpendicular angle) and stored in the patient's clinical record.

4.5 All wound care treatments shall follow evidence-based clinical practice guidelines (NPIAP/EPUAP/PPPIA for pressure injuries; Society for Vascular Surgery/American Venous Forum for venous ulcers; WOCN guidelines). The clinician shall document the clinical rationale for the selected treatment approach.

4.6 The assessing clinician shall ensure that OASIS wound items (M1311–M1350) are coded accurately and are consistent with the narrative wound assessment documentation in the visit note per CL-OA-007. Any discrepancy between the OASIS wound coding and the wound assessment documentation is a documentation integrity failure subject to CO-DC-001.

4.7 Wound status shall be monitored at each nursing visit, with a formal comparison to baseline status at minimum every 2 weeks. If the wound is not showing expected progress within 2–4 weeks of treatment, the RN shall: (a) reassess the treatment approach; (b) consult the Director of Nursing; (c) notify the physician with a recommendation for treatment modification; (d) consider wound care specialist referral if available.

4.8 The Director of Nursing shall ensure that all RNs and LVNs providing wound care have demonstrated competency in wound assessment, staging, measurement, treatment techniques, and OASIS wound item coding.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Initial Wound Assessment at SOC

6.2 Ongoing Wound Assessment and Treatment

6.3 OASIS Wound Item Coding Integration

6.4 Patient and Caregiver Education

6.5 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors will select patients with active wounds and compare: (a) the wound assessment documentation with the OASIS wound item coding — inconsistencies are cited; (b) wound care orders with treatment provided — any deviation from the ordered treatment is a deficiency; (c) wound measurement trends to evaluate whether the wound is progressing and whether the treatment plan is responsive; (d) wound staging accuracy — reverse-staging is always cited. OASIS validation auditors will specifically target wound items because wound status directly impacts PDGM payment classification.

8.3 Common Failure Points

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All RNs and LVNs providing wound care shall receive wound care competency training at hire and annually thereafter, including: NPIAP staging; wound measurement technique; wound photography standards; OASIS wound item coding; and evidence-based treatment protocols.

10.2 Wound care competency shall be validated through: (a) written knowledge assessment (≥80% passing score); (b) wound assessment skills demonstration (using wound models or clinical observation).

10.3 All personnel within scope shall acknowledge this policy within 14 calendar days.

CL-SD-012 — Medication Management & Administration

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy establishes the standards for medication administration, medication management, medication storage assessment, medication reconciliation, adverse drug reaction identification and reporting, and patient/caregiver medication education at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. Medication management is a core component of skilled nursing in the home health setting and is integral to virtually every patient encounter. Medication errors — including wrong dose, wrong medication, wrong route, omitted doses, drug-drug interactions, and failure to identify adverse reactions — are among the leading causes of preventable patient harm in home health. This policy ensures that all medication-related activities performed by agency staff comply with 42 CFR § 484.60 (plan of care conformance), 42 CFR § 484.75 (skilled professional services), the California Nursing Practice Act, and CMS Home Health Benefit Manual coverage requirements for skilled nursing related to medication management.

3. Scope

This policy applies to:

All registered nurses (RNs) providing medication administration and management services

All licensed vocational nurses (LVNs) administering medications within their California BPC § 2859 scope

Home health aides — to the extent of their observation and reporting obligations regarding medication compliance (HHAs do not administer medications)

The Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager

Physicians and allowed practitioners ordering medications

Revenue Cycle staff verifying medication-related documentation supports billed services

This policy does not govern IV medication administration (CL-SD-010), high-risk medication safety protocols (RM-PS-005), or medication reconciliation at transitions of care (CL-SD-013), though it cross-references each.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 All medication administration by agency staff shall be authorized by a current, valid physician order specifying: drug name, dose, route, frequency, and indication. No medication shall be administered without a physician order per CL-CP-003.

4.2 All medication administration shall follow the "Five Rights" verification protocol: right patient, right medication, right dose, right route, and right time. Additionally, the clinician shall verify the right documentation and right reason (the "Seven Rights" standard adopted by the agency).

4.3 At each skilled nursing visit, the assigned RN or LVN shall perform a comprehensive medication review including: (a) verification of the patient's actual medication-taking behavior against the prescribed regimen; (b) assessment of medication storage conditions; (c) identification of new, changed, or discontinued medications since the last visit; (d) assessment for adverse drug reactions, side effects, and drug-drug interactions; (e) assessment of the patient's and caregiver's understanding of the medication regimen; (f) documentation of all findings per Section 6.

4.4 When medication discrepancies are identified — including discrepancies between the prescribed regimen and actual patient behavior, between the agency's medication list and the pharmacy profile, or between the plan of care medication list and the current physician orders — the RN shall initiate reconciliation per CL-SD-013, notify the physician within 24 hours, and document all findings and actions.

4.5 All suspected adverse drug reactions (ADRs) shall be immediately reported to the physician, documented in the clinical record, and reported through the agency's incident reporting system per RM-ER-002. Serious ADRs that meet FDA MedWatch criteria shall be reported to the FDA.

4.6 Medication education shall be provided to the patient and caregiver at SOC and reinforced at each visit, covering: the purpose of each medication, dose and frequency, route of administration, common side effects, signs of adverse reactions to report, storage requirements, and the importance of adherence.

4.7 The agency shall not store, dispense, or pre-pour medications for patients. The agency's role is to administer medications as ordered during visits, assess the patient's medication management capability, provide education, and coordinate with the physician and pharmacy to resolve medication issues.

4.8 For patients identified as at risk for medication non-adherence (polypharmacy, cognitive impairment, complex regimens, history of non-compliance), the RN shall develop and implement a medication management plan as part of the plan of care, including interventions such as: medication organizer setup assistance, simplified regimen coordination with the physician, caregiver training, and pharmacy coordination.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Medication Review at Each Skilled Nursing Visit

6.2 Medication Administration During Visits

6.3 Medication Education

6.4 Medication Non-Adherence Management

6.5 Controlled Substance Documentation

6.6 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors conducting a standard survey under SOM Appendix B will specifically verify under Tag G160 and G184:

Evidence that medication management is a component of the skilled nursing visit. Surveyors will select patient records and verify that the visit note documents a medication review — not merely a notation that "meds reviewed" but a substantive assessment of the patient's medication regimen, adherence, and any identified concerns. Superficial medication documentation is a common deficiency.

Evidence that medication lists are current and consistent. Surveyors will compare the plan of care medication list, the visit note medication documentation, and the OASIS medication items. Inconsistencies across these documents are cited as documentation integrity failures.

Evidence that medication education is individualized and documented. Surveyors may interview patients about their understanding of their medications. If the patient cannot describe the purpose of their medications and the clinical record does not document education, a deficiency will be cited.

Evidence that adverse drug reactions are identified and managed. Surveyors will look for clinical records where ADR symptoms are documented in visit notes but there is no corresponding physician notification or follow-up action.

Evidence that medication errors are reported and investigated. Surveyors will review the agency's incident reporting system for medication error trends and verify that root cause analysis is conducted for significant errors.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 CMS Guidance

9.3 State Requirements

9.4 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All RNs and LVNs shall receive competency-validated training on medication management and administration requirements within 14 calendar days of hire and annually thereafter, including: (a) the Seven Rights protocol; (b) medication review documentation standards; (c) ADR identification and reporting; (d) high-alert medication protocols per RM-PS-005; (e) medication non-adherence assessment and intervention; (f) controlled substance documentation requirements; (g) medication education documentation standards.

10.2 All RNs and LVNs shall demonstrate competency in medication administration — including injection technique, medication verification, and ADR recognition — through skills evaluation at hire and annually.

10.3 All personnel within scope of this policy shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix A) within 14 calendar days of the policy effective date, any revision, or new hire/appointment.

10.4 The Director of Nursing shall maintain training completion and competency records for all clinicians and shall report non-compliance to the Administrator within 7 calendar days of any training deadline.

11. Version Control

11.1 This policy is maintained under the agency's enterprise policy lifecycle management system per policy EN-LC-001.

11.2 Only the most current approved version of this policy, as reflected in the policy header, is valid for any operational, compliance, or regulatory purpose. All superseded versions must be archived and clearly marked as "SUPERSEDED — NOT FOR USE."

11.3 Any substantive revision to this policy requires: (a) review and approval by the Governing Body, documented in meeting minutes; (b) re-acknowledgment by all personnel within scope within 14 calendar days of the revised effective date; (c) update to the enterprise policy index per EN-TG-001.

11.4 Non-substantive revisions (formatting, typographical corrections, updated cross-references) may be approved by the Administrator with notification to the Governing Body at the next regular meeting.

CL-SD-013 — Medication Reconciliation at Transitions

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy mandates medication reconciliation at every transition of care for all patients served by Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. Transitions of care — including Start of Care (SOC), Resumption of Care (ROC) following an inpatient stay, Transfer to an inpatient facility, and Discharge — represent the highest-risk periods for medication errors. Studies consistently demonstrate that medication discrepancies occur in 50–70% of care transitions, and that unresolved discrepancies are a leading cause of adverse drug events, emergency department visits, and hospital readmissions. Medication reconciliation is the structured clinical process of comparing the patient's current medication orders against all medications the patient is actually taking — including those from prior care settings — to identify and resolve discrepancies before they cause patient harm. This policy ensures that Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. performs rigorous, documented medication reconciliation at every transition, compliant with 42 CFR § 484.60, The Joint Commission National Patient Safety Goals (if accredited), and CMS quality measurement expectations.

3. Scope

This policy applies to:

All registered nurses conducting SOC, ROC, transfer, and discharge assessments

All clinicians who identify medication changes during an episode

The Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager

Physicians and allowed practitioners whose orders are the subject of reconciliation

Pharmacy partners providing medication profiles

Revenue Cycle staff who rely on accurate medication documentation for coding

This policy governs medication reconciliation specifically at transitions of care. Ongoing medication review during skilled visits is governed by CL-SD-012. OASIS medication items (distinguishing prescribed regimen from actual patient behavior) are governed by CL-OA-014.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 Medication reconciliation shall be performed and documented at every transition of care: (a) Start of Care (SOC); (b) Resumption of Care (ROC) following any inpatient stay; (c) Transfer to an inpatient facility; and (d) Discharge from home health services.

4.2 The medication reconciliation process shall compare a minimum of three medication sources: (a) the patient's/caregiver's reported medication list (what the patient is actually taking); (b) the physician-ordered medication list (what should be prescribed); (c) at least one external verification source — the hospital/SNF discharge medication list, the pharmacy medication profile, or the referring physician's medication records.

4.3 All discrepancies identified during reconciliation shall be classified as: (a) intentional — the physician made a documented change; or (b) unintentional — a discrepancy requiring physician resolution. Unintentional discrepancies shall be communicated to the physician within 24 hours for resolution.

4.4 The reconciled, physician-verified medication list shall become the official medication list in the patient's plan of care and shall serve as the baseline for all subsequent medication reviews per CL-SD-012.

4.5 The patient and caregiver shall receive an updated, written medication list at the completion of each reconciliation event.

4.6 Medication reconciliation at SOC and ROC shall be completed before the first non-SOC/ROC visit to ensure that all subsequent clinicians are working from an accurate, reconciled medication list.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Medication Reconciliation at Start of Care (SOC)

6.2 Medication Reconciliation at Resumption of Care (ROC)

6.3 Medication Reconciliation at Transfer to Inpatient Facility

6.4 Medication Reconciliation at Discharge

6.5 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors will specifically examine medication reconciliation at transitions under Tag G160 (42 CFR § 484.60). They will:

Select patients who have had a recent hospitalization (ROC) and verify that the agency performed medication reconciliation at the ROC visit — comparing the discharge medication list with the prior home health medication list and the patient's actual medications at home.

Verify that discrepancies were identified, documented, and resolved with the physician.

Interview patients about whether they received an updated medication list and whether they understand their medications.

Compare the agency's medication list in the plan of care against the patient's actual medications at the bedside — inconsistencies are cited as documentation integrity failures and patient safety concerns.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 CMS Guidance

9.3 Clinical Practice Standards

9.4 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All RNs shall receive competency-validated training on medication reconciliation at hire and annually thereafter, including: the three-source comparison methodology; discrepancy identification and classification; physician communication requirements; documentation standards; and the use of the Medication Reconciliation Worksheet.

10.2 All personnel within scope shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix B) within 14 calendar days of the effective date, any revision, or new hire.

11. Version Control

11.1 This policy is maintained per EN-LC-001. Only the most current approved version is valid. Superseded versions shall be archived as "SUPERSEDED — NOT FOR USE."

11.2 Substantive revisions require Governing Body approval. Non-substantive revisions may be approved by the Administrator.

CL-SD-014 — Pain Assessment & Management

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy defines the requirements for pain screening, assessment, reassessment, and individualized pain management planning for all patients served by Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. Pain is a subjective experience that significantly affects the patient's functional status, quality of life, participation in rehabilitation, medication adherence, sleep, appetite, and overall recovery trajectory. Inadequate pain assessment is a patient safety failure; inadequate pain management is a clinical quality failure. Conversely, over-reliance on opioid analgesics without a comprehensive pain management approach contributes to the national opioid crisis and requires vigilant clinical oversight. This policy ensures that pain is assessed at every patient encounter using validated tools, that pain management is individualized and multimodal, and that all pain-related clinical activities are documented in compliance with 42 CFR § 484.60, CMS OASIS pain item requirements, and evidence-based clinical practice guidelines.

3. Scope

This policy applies to all clinical disciplines (RN, LVN, PT, OT, SLP, MSW, HHA) — as each discipline has a role in pain identification, assessment, and/or management within their scope. Specifically: RNs conduct comprehensive pain assessments and manage pharmacologic and non-pharmacologic interventions; LVNs conduct focused pain assessments and administer pain medications; PTs, OTs, and SLPs assess pain within the context of their therapeutic interventions; MSWs assess psychosocial aspects of pain; HHAs observe and report pain indicators.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 Pain shall be screened at every patient encounter by every clinical discipline — using a validated, age-appropriate, and cognitively appropriate pain screening tool. Pain is the "fifth vital sign" and shall be documented with the same rigor and consistency as blood pressure, pulse, temperature, and respiration.

4.2 At SOC and at each OASIS assessment time point, the assigned RN shall conduct a comprehensive pain assessment including: (a) pain location(s); (b) pain intensity using a validated scale; (c) pain quality (sharp, dull, burning, aching, throbbing, stabbing); (d) pain onset, duration, and pattern (constant, intermittent, activity-related); (e) aggravating and alleviating factors; (f) impact of pain on function (mobility, ADLs, sleep, appetite, mood); (g) current pain management strategies — both pharmacologic and non-pharmacologic; (h) patient's pain management goals; (i) assessment for risk factors for opioid misuse (if applicable) per RM-PS-005.

4.3 Pain management shall be multimodal — incorporating pharmacologic interventions (per physician order), non-pharmacologic interventions (positioning, heat/cold therapy, relaxation techniques, guided imagery, therapeutic exercise), and patient education on self-management strategies.

4.4 Pain reassessment shall be conducted: (a) at every skilled visit; (b) within 1 hour of any pain medication administration during a visit; (c) following any non-pharmacologic pain intervention; (d) whenever the patient reports a change in pain pattern, intensity, or location.

4.5 All OASIS pain items shall be coded accurately and consistently with the narrative pain assessment documentation in the visit note per CL-OA-007.

4.6 The physician shall be notified within 24 hours when: (a) the patient's pain is uncontrolled despite current interventions; (b) a change in pain medication is indicated; (c) the patient reports new pain or a significant change in existing pain; (d) the patient is exhibiting signs of opioid adverse effects (sedation, respiratory depression, constipation, confusion).

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Pain Screening at Every Visit

6.2 Comprehensive Pain Assessment at SOC and OASIS Time Points

6.3 Pain Reassessment and Intervention Monitoring

6.4 Opioid-Specific Monitoring

6.5 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors will verify that pain assessment is a component of every skilled visit, that validated tools are used, that pain management is individualized and not solely pharmacologic, and that there is evidence of reassessment following interventions. Surveyors may interview patients about their pain experience and whether staff asked about their pain. Failure to assess pain at every visit is a commonly cited deficiency.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 CMS Guidance

9.3 Clinical Practice Standards

9.4 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All clinical disciplines shall receive training on pain assessment and management requirements within 14 calendar days of hire and annually thereafter, covering: validated pain assessment tools and their appropriate application; comprehensive pain assessment elements; multimodal pain management strategies; opioid monitoring requirements; and pain documentation standards.

10.2 All personnel within scope shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix B) within 14 calendar days of the effective date, revision, or hire.

CL-SD-015 — Fall Risk Assessment & Prevention

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy establishes the fall risk screening, assessment, intervention planning, and documentation requirements for all patients served by Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. Falls are the leading cause of injury-related death and hospitalization among older adults, and fall prevention is a core quality measure in home health. Falls in the home health population are tracked through OASIS-derived quality measures and are publicly reported on CMS Home Health Compare. A fall resulting in hospitalization or major injury during the home health episode is an adverse event that directly impacts the agency's quality scores, star ratings, and survey outcomes. This policy ensures that every patient receives a standardized fall risk assessment at admission and at defined intervals, that individualized fall prevention interventions are implemented and documented, that falls are reported and investigated, and that fall prevention education is provided to every patient and caregiver.

3. Scope

This policy applies to all clinical disciplines (RN, LVN, PT, OT, SLP, MSW, HHA), as every clinician has a role in fall risk identification and prevention. PTs and OTs have primary responsibility for functional mobility and balance assessment; RNs have primary responsibility for medication-related fall risk assessment and comprehensive risk factor identification; all disciplines are responsible for environmental safety assessment and patient education.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 Fall risk screening shall be performed at every patient encounter by every clinical discipline — if a patient reports a fall or near-fall since the last visit, or if the clinician observes any change in the patient's mobility, balance, or cognitive status, a comprehensive fall risk reassessment shall be initiated.

4.2 A comprehensive fall risk assessment shall be completed by the assigned RN at SOC, at each OASIS assessment time point, following any fall, and whenever a significant change in the patient's condition affects fall risk. The assessment shall use a validated fall risk assessment tool (e.g., Morse Fall Scale, Timed Up and Go test, or equivalent) and shall include all risk factors identified in Section 6.2.

4.3 An individualized Fall Prevention Plan shall be developed as part of the plan of care for every patient identified as at-risk for falls. The plan shall address modifiable risk factors and shall be reviewed and updated at each recertification and following any fall.

4.4 All falls occurring during the home health episode — whether witnessed by agency staff, reported by the patient/caregiver, or identified through clinical assessment — shall be documented, reported to the physician within 24 hours, assessed for injury, and reported through the agency's incident reporting system per RM-ER-002 and adverse event reporting per QA-AE-001.

4.5 Fall prevention education shall be provided to the patient and caregiver at SOC and reinforced at each visit, including: personal risk factors, home safety modifications, safe transfer and ambulation techniques, appropriate footwear, medication-related fall risk, and when to call for assistance.

4.6 OASIS fall-related items shall be coded accurately and consistently with the clinical record per CL-OA-007.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Fall Risk Screening at Every Visit

6.2 Comprehensive Fall Risk Assessment

6.3 Fall Prevention Plan Development

6.4 Fall Reporting and Post-Fall Assessment

6.5 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors will verify that fall risk is assessed at admission using a validated tool, that a fall prevention plan exists for at-risk patients, that falls are documented and reported, and that the agency tracks falls as a quality measure. Home Health Compare publicly reports the fall rate — a high fall rate damages the agency's star rating and public profile.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 CMS Guidance

9.3 Clinical Practice Standards

9.4 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All clinical disciplines shall receive training on fall risk assessment and prevention at hire and annually, including: validated fall risk assessment tools; FRID identification; environmental safety assessment; fall prevention education techniques; post-fall assessment and reporting requirements.

10.2 All personnel within scope shall acknowledge this policy within 14 calendar days.

CL-SD-016 — Infection Prevention & Control

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy defines the agency's infection prevention and control (IPC) program, including standard precautions, transmission-based precautions, hand hygiene requirements, personal protective equipment (PPE) use, sharps and biohazardous waste management, infection surveillance and reporting, and patient and staff education. Infection prevention is a Condition of Participation under 42 CFR § 484.70, which requires the agency to maintain an infection prevention and control program that is consistent with accepted standards of practice and is designed to prevent the development and transmission of communicable diseases and infections. The home environment presents unique IPC challenges — clinicians provide care in settings they do not control, patients may have infectious conditions requiring transmission-based precautions, and the risk of cross-contamination between patients is a direct function of staff compliance with standard precautions. This policy ensures that Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. maintains a rigorous, evidence-based IPC program compliant with 42 CFR § 484.70, CDC guidelines, OSHA Bloodborne Pathogen Standard, and California Department of Public Health reporting requirements.

3. Scope

This policy applies to all personnel who have direct or indirect contact with patients or patient care materials — including all clinical disciplines (RN, LVN, PT, OT, SLP, MSW, HHA), administrative staff who enter patient homes, and contracted clinical staff.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 Standard Precautions shall be followed by all agency staff at every patient encounter. Standard Precautions are the minimum infection prevention practices that apply to all patient care, regardless of suspected or confirmed infection status, and include: (a) hand hygiene; (b) use of personal protective equipment (PPE) based on anticipated exposure; (c) respiratory hygiene/cough etiquette; (d) safe injection practices; (e) safe handling of potentially contaminated equipment or surfaces; (f) sharps safety and disposal.

4.2 Hand hygiene shall be performed: (a) immediately before direct patient contact; (b) before donning gloves for an aseptic procedure; (c) after removal of gloves; (d) after contact with body fluids, non-intact skin, or wound dressings; (e) after contact with the patient's immediate environment; (f) between different care activities on the same patient when moving from a contaminated to a clean body site. Alcohol-based hand rub (ABHR) with ≥60% alcohol is the preferred method when hands are not visibly soiled. Soap and water shall be used when hands are visibly soiled, after caring for patients with Clostridioides difficile or norovirus, and when ABHR is not available.

4.3 Transmission-Based Precautions shall be implemented in addition to Standard Precautions when caring for patients with known or suspected communicable diseases: (a) Contact Precautions — for MRSA, VRE, C. difficile, scabies, and other contact-transmitted organisms; (b) Droplet Precautions — for influenza, pertussis, and respiratory viruses transmitted by large respiratory droplets; (c) Airborne Precautions — for tuberculosis, measles, varicella, and other airborne-transmitted organisms. The specific precautions for each condition shall follow current CDC guidelines.

4.4 PPE shall be available to all staff and used according to the anticipated exposure: (a) gloves for contact with blood, body fluids, non-intact skin, mucous membranes, and contaminated surfaces; (b) gowns for procedures likely to generate splashes or when caring for patients on contact precautions; (c) masks and eye protection for procedures that may generate splashes or sprays; (d) N95 respirators for airborne precautions (all staff on airborne precaution assignment must be fit-tested per OSHA requirements).

4.5 All sharps (needles, lancets, scalpels) shall be disposed of immediately after use in a puncture-resistant sharps container. Sharps containers shall be provided to patients receiving injectable medications at home and shall be disposed of per California Health and Safety Code requirements.

4.6 All biohazardous waste generated during home visits shall be managed per OSHA Bloodborne Pathogen Standard (29 CFR § 1910.1030) and RM-PS-002. Clinical waste shall be contained in appropriate biohazard bags, transported safely, and disposed of through the agency's contracted biohazardous waste disposal service.

4.7 The agency shall maintain an infection surveillance program per QA-SM-002 that includes: (a) tracking patient infections (urinary tract infections, wound infections, respiratory infections, CLABSI); (b) trending infection data to identify patterns; (c) reporting notifiable communicable diseases to the California Department of Public Health within required timeframes; (d) reporting infection data to the QAPI program for analysis and performance improvement.

4.8 All staff shall receive initial and annual infection prevention training per HR-TD-001, including: standard precautions, hand hygiene, PPE donning and doffing, sharps safety, bloodborne pathogen exposure procedures, and transmission-based precautions.

4.9 Exposure incidents (needlestick injuries, blood/body fluid exposures) shall be managed per OSHA post-exposure procedures: (a) immediate wound care; (b) reporting to the Director of Nursing within 2 hours; (c) medical evaluation within 2 hours per the agency's occupational health protocol; (d) incident report per RM-ER-002.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Standard Precautions at Every Visit

6.2 Transmission-Based Precautions

6.3 Sharps Safety and Biohazardous Waste

6.4 Exposure Incident Management

6.5 Infection Surveillance and Reporting

6.6 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors under Tag G306 (42 CFR § 484.70) will verify: (a) the agency has a written IPC program; (b) staff can describe and demonstrate standard precautions; (c) hand hygiene supplies (ABHR) are carried by staff; (d) transmission-based precautions are in place for qualifying patients; (e) sharps disposal and biohazardous waste management comply with OSHA; (f) infection surveillance data is tracked and reported to QAPI; (g) staff have received IPC training and N95 fit testing as applicable.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 CMS Guidance

9.3 Clinical Practice Standards

9.4 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All staff shall receive IPC training at hire and annually per HR-TD-001, covering: standard precautions, hand hygiene technique, PPE selection and use, transmission-based precautions, sharps safety, bloodborne pathogen exposure management, and infection reporting.

10.2 All staff assigned to patients on airborne precautions shall be N95 fit-tested at hire and annually per OSHA requirements.

10.3 All personnel within scope shall acknowledge this policy within 14 calendar days.

CL-SD-017 — Patient Education & Self-Management

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy requires individualized patient and caregiver education with documented learning assessments and outcomes at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. Patient education is not ancillary to clinical care — it is a skilled service, a core component of the plan of care, and the primary mechanism through which patients transition from dependency on home health services to independent self-management. A patient who is discharged without the knowledge and skills to manage their condition will return — to the emergency department, to the hospital, or to another episode of home health. Effective education reduces rehospitalization, improves patient outcomes, enhances patient satisfaction, and supports the skilled nature of every visit. This policy ensures that education is planned, individualized, culturally appropriate, delivered using evidence-based adult learning principles, assessed for effectiveness, and documented with the specificity required by CMS surveyors and ADR auditors.

3. Scope

This policy applies to all clinical disciplines — each discipline is responsible for education within their scope: RNs for disease management, medication, safety; PTs for mobility and exercise programs; OTs for ADL techniques and adaptive equipment; SLPs for communication strategies and safe swallowing; MSWs for community resources and psychosocial coping; HHAs for reinforcing education provided by skilled disciplines.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 An individualized Education Plan shall be developed as part of the plan of care for every patient at SOC, identifying: (a) learning needs based on the comprehensive assessment; (b) learning goals; (c) education topics; (d) the patient's and caregiver's preferred learning style (visual, verbal, demonstration, written materials); (e) learning barriers (literacy, language, cognition, vision, hearing, cultural factors); (f) strategies to address identified barriers.

4.2 Patient education shall be provided at every skilled visit, integrated into the clinical care — not delivered as a separate, unrelated activity. Education shall be directly relevant to the visit's clinical purpose and the patient's current status and needs.

4.3 All education shall be documented with specificity sufficient to demonstrate the skilled nature of the teaching. Documentation shall include: (a) the specific topic taught; (b) the teaching method used (verbal instruction, demonstration, teach-back, written materials, video); (c) the patient's/caregiver's response and demonstrated understanding; (d) areas requiring reinforcement; (e) the plan for follow-up education.

4.4 The teach-back method shall be the primary assessment technique: after teaching, the clinician asks the patient/caregiver to demonstrate or explain in their own words what they learned. The teach-back response shall be documented.

4.5 Education materials shall be provided in the patient's primary language per OP-PA-003 and at a literacy level appropriate to the patient's comprehension. Materials shall be culturally appropriate per OP-PA-004.

4.6 Education effectiveness shall be evaluated at each visit by assessing whether the patient can demonstrate or verbalize the taught content. If the patient is not demonstrating learning, the clinician shall modify the education approach (different learning method, shorter sessions, caregiver involvement, simplified content) and document the modification.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Learning Needs Assessment at SOC

6.2 Education Delivery at Every Visit

6.3 Education Documentation

6.4 Discharge Education

6.5 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors will review visit notes for evidence of patient education — they specifically look for individualized education (not generic), teach-back documentation, and education adapted to the patient's learning needs. Surveyors frequently interview patients to verify that education was provided. ADR auditors will examine whether education documentation supports the skilled nature of the visit — visits where the only skilled component is education must demonstrate that the education required the skills of a licensed clinician.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 CMS Guidance

9.3 Clinical Practice Standards

9.4 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All clinical disciplines shall receive training on patient education standards within 14 calendar days of hire and annually, including: adult learning principles; the teach-back method; health literacy; education documentation standards; and cultural competency in education.

10.2 All personnel within scope shall acknowledge this policy within 14 calendar days.

CL-SD-018 — Diabetic Management & Monitoring

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy establishes the assessment, education, monitoring, and documentation standards for the management of patients with diabetes mellitus at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. Diabetes is one of the most prevalent diagnoses in the home health population, affecting approximately 30% of all Medicare home health beneficiaries. Effective diabetic management in the home requires coordinated monitoring of blood glucose, medication management (including insulin administration and oral hypoglycemic agents), dietary education, foot care, complication screening, and self-management training — all of which are skilled services that directly impact patient outcomes, hospitalization rates, and quality measure performance. Poor glycemic management in home health is associated with increased emergency department visits, hospital readmissions, diabetic wound complications, and patient morbidity. This policy ensures that all patients with diabetes receive comprehensive, evidence-based diabetic care integrated into the plan of care.

3. Scope

This policy applies to all RNs and LVNs providing skilled nursing services to diabetic patients, PTs and OTs whose interventions may affect glycemic control (exercise programs), the Director of Nursing, and Revenue Cycle staff relying on diabetic documentation for coding and billing.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 All patients with a diagnosis of diabetes mellitus shall receive a comprehensive diabetic assessment at SOC, including: (a) diabetes type (Type 1, Type 2, gestational, other); (b) current diabetic medication regimen (insulin, oral agents, injectables) with verification of actual adherence per CL-SD-012; (c) blood glucose monitoring frequency and recent values; (d) HbA1c level (most recent; physician order for new HbA1c if not available within past 3 months); (e) hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia history; (f) diabetic complications screening — retinopathy, nephropathy, neuropathy, peripheral vascular disease, foot ulcers; (g) dietary assessment — current dietary habits, carbohydrate management, nutritional status; (h) foot assessment — skin integrity, sensation (monofilament testing if available), pulses, deformity, ulceration, callus, toenail condition; (i) patient's and caregiver's current knowledge of diabetes self-management.

4.2 A diabetic management plan shall be developed as part of the plan of care, including: (a) physician-ordered blood glucose monitoring schedule; (b) target blood glucose range per physician parameters; (c) insulin administration schedule (if applicable) with sliding scale or correction factor if ordered; (d) hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia recognition and response protocols; (e) dietary goals; (f) foot care regimen; (g) self-management education goals; (h) exercise recommendations coordinated with PT if applicable.

4.3 At each skilled nursing visit for a diabetic patient, the RN or LVN shall: (a) assess blood glucose per the ordered monitoring schedule; (b) review the patient's blood glucose log since the last visit; (c) assess for symptoms of hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia; (d) assess medication adherence for diabetic medications; (e) perform a focused foot assessment (visual inspection of feet — skin integrity, color, warmth, edema, signs of infection, ulceration); (f) provide targeted diabetic education; (g) document all findings.

4.4 Hypoglycemia (blood glucose <70 mg/dL) and severe hyperglycemia (blood glucose >400 mg/dL or per physician-defined parameters) shall be managed per the physician's orders and the agency's Emergency Glycemic Response Protocol (Appendix A). Physician notification is required within 4 hours for any glycemic emergency managed in the home. If the patient is symptomatic and cannot be stabilized, call 911.

4.5 Insulin administration by agency staff shall follow the medication administration requirements of CL-SD-012, with the additional requirement that blood glucose must be verified immediately before each insulin administration, and the dose must be verified against the physician's insulin order (including sliding scale if applicable) by the administering clinician.

4.6 Patient education for diabetic self-management shall be provided per CL-SD-017 and shall include: (a) blood glucose self-monitoring technique; (b) insulin self-injection technique (if applicable); (c) hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia recognition and response; (d) dietary management including carbohydrate counting or plate method; (e) foot care (daily inspection, proper footwear, when to seek medical attention); (f) sick day management rules; (g) importance of follow-up with endocrinology or primary care.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Diabetic Assessment at SOC

6.2 Ongoing Diabetic Monitoring at Each Visit

6.3 Insulin Administration

6.4 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors will verify that diabetic management is integrated into the plan of care, that blood glucose monitoring is performed per physician orders, that the agency identifies and manages glycemic emergencies, and that patient education supports self-management. ADR auditors will specifically examine whether diabetic visit documentation supports the skilled nature of the service — glucose checks alone, without clinical analysis and education, may not meet the skilled service threshold.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 Clinical Practice Standards

9.3 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All RNs and LVNs shall receive diabetic management competency training at hire and annually, including: comprehensive diabetic assessment; blood glucose monitoring and interpretation; insulin administration including sliding scale calculation; hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia recognition and management; diabetic foot assessment technique; and diabetic self-management education strategies.

10.2 All personnel within scope shall acknowledge this policy within 14 calendar days.

CL-CP-007 — Transfer & Referral Procedures

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy establishes the requirements for transferring patients from Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. to inpatient facilities (hospitals, skilled nursing facilities, inpatient rehabilitation facilities, long-term acute care hospitals), to other home health agencies, to hospice programs, and for receiving incoming transfers from other care settings. Transfer is a high-risk transition event that directly affects patient safety, clinical continuity, OASIS data integrity, billing compliance, and quality measure performance. An improperly executed transfer — where clinical information is incomplete, the receiving facility is not informed of the patient's current clinical status and medications, or the transfer OASIS is not completed — results in fragmented care, medication errors, preventable readmissions, and regulatory deficiencies. This policy ensures that every transfer is clinically coordinated, documented with the required clinical summary, accompanied by a warm handoff to the receiving provider, and compliant with CMS OASIS transfer assessment requirements, 42 CFR § 484.60 (care coordination), and the agency's documentation and billing alignment standards.

3. Scope

This policy applies to:

All registered nurses managing active patient caseloads

All clinical disciplines involved in the patient's care at the time of transfer

The Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager

Clinical Coordinators managing transfer scheduling and OASIS documentation

Medical Records staff managing transfer documentation

Revenue Cycle staff managing episode billing affected by transfers

Operations staff coordinating with receiving facilities

The attending physician who must be notified of and, where applicable, order the transfer

This policy governs all outgoing transfers from home health to another care setting and all incoming transfers (resumption of care) from an inpatient setting back to home health. It does not govern discharge (CL-CP-006) unless the transfer constitutes a permanent discharge from home health services.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 When a patient is transferred from home health to an inpatient facility, the agency shall ensure that: (a) the attending physician is notified and, where clinically appropriate, orders or approves the transfer; (b) a comprehensive clinical summary is prepared and transmitted to the receiving facility; (c) a warm handoff (direct clinician-to-clinician communication) occurs with the receiving care team; (d) the transfer OASIS assessment is completed within CMS-required timeframes; (e) the patient's medication list, active diagnoses, current plan of care, and advance directive status are communicated to the receiving facility; (f) the patient and caregiver are informed of the transfer and the reason for it.

4.2 The clinical transfer summary shall include, at minimum: (a) the patient's current diagnoses and relevant medical history; (b) the current, reconciled medication list including recent changes; (c) the reason for transfer; (d) the patient's functional status at the time of transfer; (e) all active treatments and interventions; (f) relevant laboratory and diagnostic results; (g) advance directive status; (h) the attending physician's name and contact information; (i) allergies and adverse drug reactions; (j) the patient's emergency contact information; (k) any known infection or transmission-based precaution status.

4.3 When a patient returns to home health services following an inpatient facility stay, the agency shall treat the return as a Resumption of Care (ROC) event requiring: (a) a new comprehensive assessment including all applicable OASIS items; (b) a medication reconciliation per CL-SD-013; (c) an updated plan of care reflecting the patient's post-facility clinical status; (d) physician orders for resumed services; (e) review and update of all clinical disciplines involved in the patient's care.

4.4 Transfers to other home health agencies shall be coordinated to ensure continuity of care, including transmission of the complete clinical record (or a comprehensive summary with the patient's consent) and direct communication with the receiving agency's clinical leadership.

4.5 Transfers to hospice shall be coordinated per CL-SD-023 and shall include advance directive review, physician discussion regarding prognosis, and patient/caregiver education regarding the hospice benefit.

4.6 All transfers — inpatient, inter-agency, and hospice — shall be documented in the patient's clinical record with the date, time, reason, receiving facility or agency, clinical summary transmitted, warm handoff documentation, and OASIS completion status.

4.7 The transfer OASIS shall be completed on the day of transfer or within the CMS-defined timeframe, and transmitted to the OASIS database per CL-CA-003. Failure to complete the transfer OASIS affects quality measure calculation and billing integrity.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Transfer to Inpatient Facility

6.2 Resumption of Care (ROC) Following Inpatient Stay

6.3 Transfer to Another Home Health Agency (Inter-Agency Transfer)

6.4 Transfer to Hospice

6.5 Emergency Department Visits Without Admission

6.6 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors conducting a standard survey under SOM Appendix B will specifically verify under Tag G160 (42 CFR § 484.60):

Evidence that transfers are coordinated with receiving facilities. Surveyors will select patients who have been transferred to inpatient facilities and verify that a clinical summary was transmitted, that a warm handoff occurred, and that the physician was notified. Missing transfer documentation is a care coordination deficiency.

Evidence that the transfer OASIS is completed. Surveyors will compare transfer dates with OASIS completion dates. Missing or late transfer OASIS assessments affect quality measure accuracy and are a documentation compliance deficiency.

Evidence that ROC assessments are comprehensive and timely. Surveyors will select patients who returned from inpatient stays and verify that a full ROC assessment was completed, medications were reconciled, and the plan of care was updated to reflect post-facility status.

Evidence that medication reconciliation occurs at ROC. Surveyors and ADR auditors will specifically examine whether the agency compared the inpatient discharge medication list against the prior home health medication list and the patient's actual medications. Medication discrepancies not identified and resolved at ROC are a patient safety failure.

Evidence that patients who visit the ED without admission are clinically followed up. Surveyors will verify that ED visits are documented and that the agency assessed the patient's status and medications following the ED visit.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 CMS Guidance

9.3 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All RNs shall receive training on transfer and referral procedures within 14 calendar days of hire and annually thereafter, including: (a) the Clinical Transfer Summary content requirements; (b) the warm handoff process; (c) the transfer OASIS completion requirement and timing; (d) the ROC assessment and medication reconciliation process; (e) the distinction between transfers, discharges, and ED visits without admission for OASIS and billing purposes; (f) the inter-agency and hospice transfer processes.

10.2 All clinical disciplines shall receive orientation to transfer notification requirements (informing the assigned RN and Director of Nursing of any patient transfer) within 14 calendar days of hire.

10.3 All personnel within scope shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix B) within 14 calendar days of the effective date, any revision, or new hire.

11. Version Control

11.1 This policy is maintained under the agency's enterprise policy lifecycle management system per policy EN-LC-001.

11.2 Only the most current approved version of this policy, as reflected in the policy header, is valid for any operational, compliance, or regulatory purpose. All superseded versions must be archived and clearly marked as "SUPERSEDED — NOT FOR USE."

11.3 Any substantive revision to this policy requires: (a) review and approval by the Governing Body, documented in meeting minutes; (b) re-acknowledgment by all personnel within scope within 14 calendar days of the revised effective date; (c) update to the enterprise policy index per EN-TG-001.

11.4 Non-substantive revisions (formatting, typographical corrections, updated cross-references) may be approved by the Administrator with notification to the Governing Body at the next regular meeting.

CL-CP-008 — Physician Recertification Timing Compliance

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy enforces the 60-day physician recertification timeline with defined escalation protocols, tracking mechanisms, and accountability measures to prevent lapses in physician certification of continued eligibility for home health services at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. Under Medicare, a patient's eligibility for home health benefits must be recertified by the physician at least every 60 calendar days. Each 60-day certification period requires a signed physician recertification statement confirming that the patient continues to need skilled services, continues to be homebound, and that a plan of care has been established and is periodically reviewed. A lapse in recertification — where the 60-day period expires without a physician-signed recertification — renders all services delivered after the expiration date unbillable under Medicare, creates a False Claims Act risk if claims are submitted, and constitutes a documentation compliance failure during CMS surveys or ADR audits. This policy establishes a proactive, systematic tracking and escalation process to ensure that no recertification deadline is missed.

3. Scope

This policy applies to:

All registered nurses managing active patient caseloads responsible for initiating the recertification assessment

Clinical Coordinators responsible for tracking recertification timelines

The Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager responsible for oversight of the recertification process

Physicians and allowed practitioners who must review, sign, and date the recertification

Revenue Cycle staff who must verify recertification before claim submission

Medical Records staff who file and manage signed recertification documents

4. Policy Statement

4.1 Every patient receiving Medicare home health services shall have a physician-signed recertification on file for each 60-day certification period. The recertification must be signed by the physician before the agency submits a claim for services rendered during that certification period.

4.2 The agency shall maintain a centralized Recertification Tracking System that monitors every active patient's certification period start date, certification period end date, the recertification assessment due date window (Day 56–60), the date the recertification plan of care was transmitted to the physician, and the date the physician signature was received.

4.3 The recertification assessment — including the comprehensive OASIS assessment for the recertification time point — shall be completed between Day 56 and Day 60 of the current certification period per CL-CA-004 and CMS OASIS timing requirements.

4.4 The recertification plan of care shall be transmitted to the physician for signature no later than the last day of the current certification period (Day 60). Earlier transmission is strongly preferred to allow physician review time.

4.5 If the physician-signed recertification is not received within 7 calendar days of transmission, escalation shall be initiated per Section 6.3.

4.6 No claim shall be submitted for a certification period unless a physician-signed recertification is on file. Revenue Cycle shall implement a billing hold for any episode where the recertification signature has not been received.

4.7 If a patient no longer meets Medicare home health eligibility criteria at the time of recertification (no longer homebound, no longer requires skilled services), the clinician shall not seek recertification and shall initiate discharge planning per CL-CP-006 instead.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Recertification Tracking and Alerting

6.2 Recertification Assessment and Plan of Care Transmission

6.3 Physician Signature Follow-Up and Escalation

6.4 Recertification Documentation and Filing

6.5 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors will request clinical records and verify that a physician-signed recertification exists for every certification period in the survey look-back period. They will cross-reference certification period dates with recertification signature dates and identify any gaps. ADR auditors from the MAC will specifically deny claims where the recertification is missing, unsigned, undated, or dated outside the applicable certification period. The recertification is one of the most frequently audited Medicare home health documentation elements.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 CMS Guidance

9.3 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All RNs shall receive training on recertification timing requirements within 14 calendar days of hire and annually thereafter, including: the Day 56–60 assessment window; the recertification plan of care content requirements; the physician transmission timeline; and the billing implications of missing recertification.

10.2 Clinical Coordinators shall receive training on the Recertification Tracking System at hire and whenever the system is updated.

10.3 Revenue Cycle staff shall receive training on the billing hold process for unsigned recertifications at hire and annually.

10.4 All personnel within scope shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix A) within 14 calendar days of the effective date, any revision, or new hire.

11. Version Control

11.1 This policy is maintained per EN-LC-001. Only the most current approved version is valid. Superseded versions shall be archived as "SUPERSEDED — NOT FOR USE."

11.2 Substantive revisions require Governing Body approval. Non-substantive revisions approved by Administrator.

CL-CP-009 — Physician Order Signature Tracking & Escalation

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy establishes a systematic tracking process for all pending physician signatures on orders and plans of care at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc., with defined follow-up intervals, escalation tiers, and documentation of all outreach attempts. The physician signature is the legal authorization for every service the agency provides and every claim the agency submits. An unsigned order is an unauthorized service. An unsigned plan of care is an unbillable episode. The agency's financial viability, regulatory compliance, and clinical legality depend on a disciplined, relentless tracking system that ensures no physician signature request goes unresolved. This policy is the operational backbone of physician order management — it defines the infrastructure, timelines, responsible parties, and escalation pathways that prevent signature lapses from becoming billing compliance failures, survey deficiencies, or False Claims Act exposures.

3. Scope

This policy applies to:

Clinical Coordinators who operate the tracking system daily

All registered nurses who transmit orders and plans of care to physicians

The Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager who is responsible for escalation oversight

The Administrator who is the final escalation authority

Revenue Cycle staff who must verify signature status before claim submission

Medical Records staff who file and verify signed documents

Physicians and allowed practitioners whose signatures are tracked

This policy applies to all documents requiring physician signature including: initial plans of care (CMS-485), recertification plans of care, verbal order authentications, change orders, and any other physician-authorization document.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 The agency shall maintain a centralized Physician Order Signature Tracking System (the "Tracking System") that captures every document requiring a physician signature from the date of transmission through the date of receipt and filing.

4.2 Every document requiring a physician signature shall be entered into the Tracking System at the time of transmission to the physician's office, including: (a) the document type (initial POC, recertification POC, verbal order authentication, change order); (b) the patient's name and medical record number; (c) the physician's name, NPI, and contact information; (d) the date of transmission; (e) the method of transmission (fax, secure portal, mail, hand-delivery); (f) the signature deadline (based on document type); (g) the status (pending, follow-up initiated, received, overdue, escalated).

4.3 The Tracking System shall generate automated alerts at defined follow-up milestones: Day 5 (first follow-up alert), Day 7 (second follow-up and Director of Nursing notification), Day 14 (Director of Nursing direct contact), and Day 21 (Administrator escalation).

4.4 All follow-up attempts shall be documented in the Tracking System including the date, time, method, person contacted, and outcome of each attempt.

4.5 Revenue Cycle shall not submit any claim for any episode or service where the corresponding physician signature has not been received and verified. A pre-bill signature verification step is mandatory before every claim submission.

4.6 The Director of Nursing shall review the Tracking System at minimum weekly and shall report to the Administrator monthly on: (a) total documents pending signature; (b) average turnaround time; (c) documents overdue beyond 14 days; (d) physicians with chronic signature delays; (e) billing hold status for affected episodes.

4.7 For physicians with a pattern of chronic non-response (defined as 3 or more documents overdue beyond 14 days within a 90-day period), the Administrator shall contact the physician practice administration in writing to address the pattern and establish a corrective process.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Tracking System Entry and Initial Transmission

6.2 Follow-Up Milestones

6.3 Receipt and Verification

6.4 Weekly and Monthly Reporting

6.5 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

Surveyors will request clinical records and verify that signed physician orders and plans of care are on file for every episode and every service in the survey look-back period. ADR auditors will deny claims where signed orders or plans of care are missing. OIG investigations of home health agencies frequently target unsigned orders as evidence of inadequate physician oversight, which can escalate to False Claims Act allegations. A robust, documented tracking system demonstrating proactive follow-up is a strong compliance defense.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 Clinical Coordinators shall receive comprehensive training on the Tracking System at hire, including data entry requirements, follow-up milestone procedures, reporting generation, and escalation protocols.

10.2 All RNs shall receive training on the requirement to enter documents into the Tracking System at the time of transmission, within 14 calendar days of hire and annually.

10.3 Revenue Cycle staff shall receive training on the pre-bill signature verification process at hire and annually.

10.4 All personnel within scope shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix A) within 14 calendar days.

11. Version Control

Maintained per EN-LC-001. Substantive revisions require Governing Body approval. Non-substantive revisions approved by Administrator.
