CL-SD-019 — Cardiac Care & Monitoring

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy defines the assessment, intervention, monitoring, and documentation standards for patients with cardiac conditions at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. Cardiac diagnoses — including congestive heart failure (CHF), coronary artery disease (CAD), atrial fibrillation, post-myocardial infarction, post-cardiac surgery, hypertension, and valvular disease — constitute one of the largest clinical populations in Medicare home health. Heart failure alone accounts for the highest rate of 30-day hospital readmissions among Medicare beneficiaries, and the CMS Home Health Compare publicly reports the agency's rehospitalization rate for heart failure patients as a quality measure directly affecting star ratings. Effective cardiac care in the home requires meticulous daily weight and symptom monitoring, complex medication management (including anticoagulants, diuretics, beta-blockers, ACE inhibitors/ARBs, and antiarrhythmics), dietary sodium restriction education, activity management, and early identification of decompensation signs that, if recognized and treated promptly, can prevent an emergency department visit or hospitalization. This policy ensures that all cardiac patients receive evidence-based, protocol-driven care that reduces rehospitalization, improves functional outcomes, and is documented with the clinical specificity required by CMS and ADR auditors.

3. Scope

This policy applies to all RNs and LVNs providing skilled nursing to cardiac patients, PTs providing cardiac rehabilitation and activity progression, OTs addressing cardiac-related ADL limitations and energy conservation, the Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager, and Revenue Cycle staff relying on cardiac documentation for coding and billing.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 All patients admitted with a primary or secondary cardiac diagnosis shall receive a comprehensive cardiac assessment at SOC including: (a) cardiac history — prior MI, CHF classification (NYHA Class I–IV), surgeries, catheterizations, device implants (pacemaker, ICD, LVAD); (b) current symptoms — dyspnea (at rest and with exertion), orthopnea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea, edema, fatigue, chest pain, palpitations, dizziness, syncope; (c) baseline vital signs including orthostatic blood pressure; (d) baseline weight (obtained on the same scale, at the same time of day, in the same clothing — establish the "dry weight" with physician input); (e) cardiac auscultation — heart sounds, presence of murmurs, gallops (S3, S4); (f) lung auscultation — presence of crackles, wheezing, diminished breath sounds; (g) peripheral edema assessment — location, severity (1+ through 4+), pitting vs. non-pitting; (h) jugular venous distention assessment; (i) skin assessment — color, temperature, capillary refill, cyanosis; (j) current cardiac medication regimen review per CL-SD-012; (k) dietary assessment — sodium intake, fluid restriction compliance; (l) activity tolerance assessment; (m) patient's and caregiver's current knowledge of cardiac self-management.

4.2 A cardiac management plan shall be developed as part of the plan of care including: (a) physician-ordered daily weight monitoring protocol with defined parameters for weight gain notification (typically >2 lbs in 24 hours or >5 lbs in 1 week); (b) physician-ordered vital sign monitoring schedule; (c) medication management plan — including specific attention to diuretics, anticoagulants, beta-blockers, ACE inhibitors/ARBs, and digoxin; (d) dietary sodium restriction (typically <2,000 mg/day or per physician order) and fluid restriction if ordered; (e) activity progression and exercise tolerance goals (coordinated with PT if involved); (f) symptom monitoring and the "zone system" education (Green/Yellow/Red zones for self-management); (g) self-management education goals; (h) defined parameters for physician notification and emergency response.

4.3 At each skilled nursing visit for a cardiac patient, the RN or LVN shall: (a) assess and document vital signs including blood pressure (lying, sitting, standing if orthostatic assessment ordered), heart rate, respiratory rate, oxygen saturation, and temperature; (b) obtain and record the patient's current weight and compare to baseline and previous visit; (c) assess for heart failure decompensation signs — increased edema, weight gain, increased dyspnea, orthopnea, jugular venous distention, crackles on lung auscultation; (d) review the patient's daily weight log and symptom diary (if applicable); (e) assess medication adherence for all cardiac medications with particular attention to diuretics, anticoagulants, and antihypertensives; (f) assess dietary sodium and fluid intake compliance; (g) assess activity tolerance — compare to prior visit; (h) perform cardiac-specific education per the education plan; (i) assess for medication-related adverse effects — hypotension, bradycardia, electrolyte imbalance symptoms, bleeding (anticoagulants), digoxin toxicity signs.

4.4 The physician shall be notified within 4 hours (or sooner for emergent findings) when any of the following are identified: (a) weight gain >2 lbs in 24 hours or >5 lbs in 1 week; (b) new or worsening edema; (c) new or worsening dyspnea at rest; (d) oxygen saturation <90% (or below the patient's physician-defined threshold); (e) systolic blood pressure >180 or <90 (or per physician parameters); (f) heart rate >120 or <50 (or per physician parameters); (g) new chest pain or angina; (h) new arrhythmia detected; (i) signs of digoxin toxicity (nausea, visual disturbances, bradycardia); (j) signs of bleeding in anticoagulated patients.

4.5 If the patient exhibits signs of acute cardiac emergency (severe chest pain, acute dyspnea at rest, oxygen saturation <85%, acute altered mental status, severe hypotension, suspected MI or stroke), call 911 immediately, initiate emergency nursing interventions within scope (position patient, administer oxygen if available and ordered, aspirin per standing order if applicable), and notify the physician and Director of Nursing within 2 hours.

4.6 All cardiac patients shall receive comprehensive self-management education per CL-SD-017 covering: (a) the "zone system" for symptom self-management — Green Zone (all clear: weight stable, no symptoms), Yellow Zone (caution: weight up 2+ lbs, increased swelling, increased shortness of breath — call the agency), Red Zone (emergency: severe shortness of breath at rest, chest pain, fainting — call 911); (b) daily weight monitoring technique (same scale, same time, same clothing, log daily); (c) sodium restriction — reading food labels, avoiding high-sodium foods, meal planning; (d) fluid restriction management (if ordered); (e) medication purpose, dose, timing, and side effects for all cardiac medications; (f) activity guidelines and energy conservation; (g) when to call the agency, when to call 911; (h) the importance of follow-up appointments.

4.7 For patients on anticoagulation therapy (warfarin, enoxaparin, DOACs), follow the enhanced medication safety protocols defined in RM-PS-005 including INR monitoring schedule for warfarin patients, bleeding risk assessment at each visit, and patient education on signs of bleeding.

4.8 PT involvement shall be initiated per physician order for cardiac patients requiring supervised activity progression, cardiac rehabilitation exercise, and functional mobility training. The PT shall coordinate activity progression with the assigned RN to ensure exercise parameters align with the patient's cardiac status and physician orders.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Cardiac Assessment at SOC

6.2 Ongoing Cardiac Monitoring at Each Visit

6.3 Heart Failure Decompensation Response

6.4 Anticoagulation Management

6.5 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors will verify that cardiac patients have comprehensive assessments, that monitoring is consistent with physician orders, that patient education is individualized and documented, and that clinical changes trigger physician notification. ADR auditors will examine whether cardiac visit documentation supports skilled nursing — a visit that documents only vital signs and weight without clinical analysis does not demonstrate skilled care.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 Clinical Practice Standards

9.3 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All RNs and LVNs providing care to cardiac patients shall receive cardiac care competency training at hire and annually thereafter, including: comprehensive cardiac assessment; heart failure decompensation recognition and response; the zone system; cardiac medication management (including anticoagulants, diuretics, beta-blockers, ACE inhibitors/ARBs, digoxin); sodium restriction education techniques; and cardiac documentation standards demonstrating skilled care.

10.2 All PTs providing cardiac rehabilitation services shall receive training on cardiac exercise parameters, activity progression safety, and coordination with nursing per physician orders.

10.3 All personnel within scope shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix B) within 14 calendar days of the effective date, revision, or hire.

11. Version Control

CL-SD-020 — Respiratory Care & Management

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy establishes the standards for respiratory assessment, oxygen therapy management, pulmonary care documentation, and clinical intervention for patients with respiratory conditions at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. Respiratory diagnoses — including chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD), asthma, pneumonia, interstitial lung disease, pulmonary fibrosis, post-COVID respiratory sequelae, bronchiectasis, lung cancer, and respiratory failure managed at home — represent a significant and clinically complex segment of the Medicare home health population. Respiratory compromise carries the highest risk of rapid clinical deterioration and emergency hospitalization of any chronic condition managed in the home setting. A patient with COPD who is not managing inhaler technique correctly, who does not recognize early exacerbation signs, or whose supplemental oxygen delivery system is malfunctioning may deteriorate from stable to life-threatening within hours. The home environment adds layers of risk — isolated patients, caregiver limitations, equipment failures, and the absence of immediately available emergency clinical resources. This policy ensures that every patient with a respiratory diagnosis receives a comprehensive, evidence-based respiratory assessment at Start of Care, that all oxygen therapy is managed with precision and documented with clinical specificity sufficient to demonstrate skilled nursing care to CMS surveyors and ADR auditors, that respiratory status is monitored at every visit using objective measurement tools, and that deterioration triggers immediate escalation and physician notification. This policy implements the requirements of 42 CFR § 484.60 (plan of care conformance), 42 CFR § 484.75 (skilled professional services), and CMS Home Health Benefit Manual respiratory care coverage requirements.

3. Scope

This policy applies to:

All registered nurses (RNs) providing skilled nursing to patients with respiratory diagnoses

All licensed vocational nurses (LVNs) providing respiratory monitoring within their California BPC § 2859 scope under RN supervision

All physical therapists (PTs) providing pulmonary rehabilitation and breathing exercise programs

All occupational therapists (OTs) providing energy conservation and ADL adaptation for respiratory patients

The Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager responsible for respiratory care oversight

Clinical Coordinators managing respiratory patient visit schedules and oxygen vendor coordination

Revenue Cycle staff verifying respiratory documentation supports billed claims

Operations staff coordinating with durable medical equipment (DME) vendors for oxygen and respiratory equipment

This policy governs all skilled nursing and therapy visits for patients with active respiratory diagnoses. It does not separately govern infection prevention standards for respiratory transmissible diseases — those are addressed in CL-SD-016 (Infection Prevention & Control) and RM-EP-001 (Pandemic & Infectious Disease Response), though cross-reference is made where applicable.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 All patients admitted with a primary or secondary respiratory diagnosis shall receive a comprehensive respiratory assessment at SOC, as defined in Section 6.1, incorporating all elements necessary to establish a clinical baseline, develop an individualized respiratory management plan, and accurately complete all applicable OASIS data elements.

4.2 A respiratory management plan shall be developed as part of the physician-approved plan of care for every patient with a respiratory diagnosis, specifying: physician-ordered oxygen therapy parameters (flow rate, delivery device, hours per day); medication regimen (bronchodilators, corticosteroids, antibiotics, mucolytics); breathing exercise program; activity tolerance and energy conservation plan; patient and caregiver education goals; exacerbation recognition and response protocols; and defined parameters for physician notification and emergency response.

4.3 At each skilled nursing visit for a respiratory patient, the RN or LVN shall perform, at minimum: a comprehensive respiratory assessment per Section 6.2; objective measurement of oxygen saturation (SpO2); verification of oxygen therapy compliance and equipment function (if applicable); inhaler technique observation and correction; respiratory medication adherence assessment; and targeted respiratory self-management education.

4.4 Supplemental oxygen shall be administered only per a valid physician order specifying the oxygen flow rate (L/min), delivery device (nasal cannula, simple face mask, Venturi mask, high-flow system), and prescribed hours of use per day (continuous, with exertion only, nocturnal only). The agency shall not modify oxygen flow rates without a physician order, including during exacerbations.

4.5 Oxygen equipment shall be assessed at each visit for: proper function, cleanliness, tubing integrity, patient and caregiver competency in equipment operation, adequate oxygen supply, and safe storage. Any equipment malfunction or supply concern shall be reported to the DME vendor and documented within 24 hours.

4.6 The physician shall be notified within 4 hours (or immediately for emergent findings) when any of the following are identified: SpO2 below the patient's physician-defined threshold (or below 88% if no threshold defined); respiratory rate >24/min at rest; accessory muscle use at rest; new or significantly worsening dyspnea; new or significantly increased productive cough; change in sputum character (color, volume, consistency) suggesting infection; new or worsening fever; signs of respiratory failure (severe dyspnea, cyanosis, altered mental status, inability to complete sentences).

4.7 If the patient presents with signs of acute respiratory emergency (SpO2 <85% despite supplemental oxygen, severe respiratory distress, cyanosis, acute altered mental status), call 911 immediately. Position the patient upright, apply supplemental oxygen at the ordered rate or increase per standing physician order, and notify the physician and Director of Nursing within 2 hours.

4.8 Inhaler technique shall be assessed and corrected at every visit for patients using metered-dose inhalers (MDIs), dry powder inhalers (DPIs), or soft-mist inhalers. Incorrect inhaler technique is one of the leading causes of uncontrolled respiratory disease and preventable exacerbations. The clinician shall document the specific inhaler technique element assessed and corrected at each visit, not simply "inhaler technique reviewed."

4.9 PT involvement shall be initiated per physician order for patients requiring pulmonary rehabilitation exercises, breathing retraining (pursed-lip breathing, diaphragmatic breathing), inspiratory muscle training, functional mobility with respiratory support, and activity tolerance improvement with respiratory monitoring.

4.10 All respiratory patients shall receive a complete respiratory self-management education program per CL-SD-017, covering: exacerbation recognition and action plan; inhaler and nebulizer use technique; oxygen safety and equipment management; energy conservation techniques; pursed-lip breathing and diaphragmatic breathing; avoidance of respiratory triggers; importance of influenza and pneumococcal vaccination; smoking cessation resources (if applicable); and when to call the agency, the physician, or 911.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Comprehensive Respiratory Assessment at SOC

6.2 Ongoing Respiratory Assessment and Monitoring at Each Visit

6.3 Oxygen Therapy Management

6.4 Respiratory Exacerbation Management

6.5 PT Respiratory Services Integration

6.6 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors conducting a standard survey under SOM Appendix B will specifically verify under Tag G160 (42 CFR § 484.60) and Tag G184 (42 CFR § 484.75):

1. Evidence that the respiratory assessment is comprehensive and individualized. Surveyors will select respiratory patients and verify that the SOC assessment captures all elements of respiratory function — not merely a SpO2 value and a lung auscultation notation. A comprehensive baseline assessment is the foundation of the skilled nursing justification for the episode.

2. Evidence that oxygen therapy is managed with clinical precision. Surveyors will verify that oxygen prescriptions are on file, that SpO2 is documented at each visit with the liter flow and device noted, and that patients receiving oxygen below their threshold have documented physician notification. Surveyors will specifically look for evidence that the nurse is clinically managing the oxygen therapy rather than merely observing it.

3. Evidence that inhaler technique is taught and assessed — not merely "reviewed." This is the single most common respiratory documentation deficiency. Surveyors consistently cite "inhaler use reviewed" as insufficient — they require documentation of the specific technique elements assessed and corrected. Surveyors may ask patients to demonstrate their inhaler technique during the survey.

4. Evidence that respiratory documentation demonstrates skilled care. ADR auditors from the MAC will deny claims where respiratory visit notes consist only of vital signs and SpO2 values without clinical analysis. The clinician must demonstrate why the visit required the skills of a licensed nurse — analyzing trends, managing complex oxygen therapy, teaching and evaluating inhaler technique, and recognizing early exacerbation signs.

5. Evidence that exacerbations are recognized, managed, and documented. Surveyors will look for evidence that clinical deterioration events triggered physician notification, plan modifications, and appropriate clinical response.

6. Evidence of respiratory self-management education that builds toward independence. Surveyors will compare education documentation across visits — identical education topics at every visit without demonstrated patient progression suggests the education is not individualized or effective.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 CMS Guidance

9.3 Clinical Practice Standards

9.4 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All RNs and LVNs providing care to respiratory patients shall receive competency-validated training within 14 calendar days of hire and annually thereafter, covering: comprehensive respiratory assessment technique; SpO2 measurement and clinical interpretation; inhaler technique assessment for all device types (MDI, DPI, soft-mist, nebulizer) with hands-on device demonstration; oxygen therapy management and equipment assessment; exacerbation recognition and classification; emergency respiratory response; respiratory documentation standards demonstrating skilled care; and oxygen safety education.

10.2 All PTs providing pulmonary rehabilitation services shall receive training on SpO2 monitoring during exercise, exercise parameter adjustment based on SpO2 findings, and coordination of respiratory exercise programs with the assigned RN.

10.3 Inhaler technique competency for all respiratory medications used by the agency's patient population shall be validated by the Director of Nursing or designee at hire, using the Inhaler Technique Competency Evaluation Tool (Appendix D), before clinicians independently teach patients inhaler use.

10.4 All personnel within scope of this policy shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix C) within 14 calendar days of the policy effective date, any revision, or new hire/appointment.

10.5 The Director of Nursing shall maintain training completion and competency records for all clinicians providing respiratory care services and shall report any non-compliance to the Administrator within 7 calendar days of any training deadline.

11. Version Control

11.1 This policy is maintained under the agency's enterprise policy lifecycle management system per policy EN-LC-001.

11.2 Only the most current approved version of this policy, as reflected in the policy header, is valid for any operational, compliance, or regulatory purpose. All superseded versions must be archived and clearly marked as "SUPERSEDED — NOT FOR USE."

11.3 Any substantive revision to this policy requires: (a) review and approval by the Governing Body, documented in meeting minutes; (b) re-acknowledgment by all personnel within scope within 14 calendar days of the revised effective date; (c) update to the enterprise policy index per EN-TG-001.

11.4 Non-substantive revisions (formatting, typographical corrections, updated cross-references) may be approved by the Administrator with notification to the Governing Body at the next regular meeting.

CL-SD-021 — Pediatric Home Health Services

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy defines the age-appropriate assessment standards, service delivery requirements, family and caregiver engagement protocols, and clinical documentation expectations for pediatric patients receiving home health services at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. Pediatric home health — defined for purposes of this policy as services delivered to patients under the age of 18 — presents clinical, ethical, legal, and regulatory dimensions that are materially distinct from adult home health. Developmental stage drives every clinical decision: the assessment tools, the communication approach, the family engagement strategy, the safety considerations, and the educational methods all differ fundamentally depending on whether the patient is a neonate, an infant, a toddler, a school-age child, or an adolescent. The pediatric patient's legal framework differs as well — informed consent belongs to the parent or legal guardian (with limited exceptions for mature minors and emancipated minors under California law), mandated reporting obligations are heightened, and developmental surveillance is a required component of every assessment. This policy ensures that every pediatric patient receives services that are developmentally appropriate, family-centered, legally compliant, and documented with the clinical specificity required by CMS, the California Department of Public Health, and ADR auditors evaluating whether the service was skilled and medically necessary. This policy implements the requirements of 42 CFR § 484.60 (plan of care conformance), 42 CFR § 484.75 (skilled professional services), and California-specific pediatric home health regulations under Title 22, California Code of Regulations.

3. Scope

This policy applies to:

All registered nurses (RNs) and licensed vocational nurses (LVNs) providing skilled nursing to pediatric patients

All physical therapists (PTs) and physical therapist assistants (PTAs) providing pediatric therapy

All occupational therapists (OTs) and certified occupational therapy assistants (COTAs) providing pediatric therapy

All speech-language pathologists (SLPs) providing pediatric services

All medical social workers (MSWs) providing pediatric family support

All home health aides (HHAs) providing pediatric personal care

The Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager

HR staff ensuring pediatric-specific competency validation before pediatric assignment

The Compliance Officer for mandated reporting obligations

This policy applies to all pediatric home health patients aged 0–17 years inclusive. It is a supplemental policy — all applicable adult clinical policies (CL-CP-001 through CL-SD-019) apply to pediatric patients unless this policy specifies a pediatric-specific modification.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. shall provide pediatric home health services only when the agency has sufficient clinicians with demonstrated pediatric clinical competency to serve the patient safely and effectively. Before accepting a pediatric referral, the Director of Nursing shall verify that: (a) at least one RN with documented pediatric clinical experience is available to manage the case; (b) all disciplines ordered are staffed by clinicians with pediatric experience or supervised by a clinician with pediatric experience; (c) the agency's equipment inventory (pediatric-sized assessment tools, pediatric vital sign reference ranges, pediatric weight-based medication calculations) supports safe pediatric care delivery.

4.2 All pediatric clinical activities — assessments, interventions, education, and documentation — shall be developmentally appropriate for the patient's specific age and developmental stage. The clinician shall document the patient's chronological age and, where clinically relevant (for premature infants and patients with developmental delays), the corrected gestational age.

4.3 Informed consent for all pediatric home health services shall be obtained from the patient's parent or legally appointed guardian, except as provided by California law for emancipated minors (California Family Code § 7002) and mature minors. Consent shall be documented per CL-PR-003 (Informed Consent). For adolescent patients, age-appropriate assent shall be sought and documented, recognizing that assent is not a substitute for parental consent.

4.4 All clinicians providing pediatric services are mandatory reporters under California Penal Code § 11165.7 and shall comply with the agency's mandatory abuse reporting policy (HR-ER-009) and patient rights policy (CL-PR-006) for the identification and reporting of suspected child abuse, neglect, or exploitation. The threshold for reporting suspected child abuse in pediatric home health is lower than in adult settings — any reasonable suspicion, without requiring proof, triggers the mandatory reporting obligation.

4.5 The plan of care for every pediatric patient shall include: (a) developmental surveillance and milestone monitoring appropriate to the patient's age; (b) family and caregiver education and training as primary partners in care; (c) age-appropriate patient education for pediatric patients with the capacity to understand; (d) growth and nutrition monitoring; (e) immunization status documentation; (f) school and developmental program coordination (for school-age children) where applicable.

4.6 Vital sign assessment in pediatric patients shall use age-appropriate reference ranges. Normal vital signs differ significantly across pediatric age groups — reference ranges for neonates, infants, toddlers, school-age children, and adolescents shall be used. No clinician shall assess pediatric vital signs without knowledge of age-appropriate normal ranges for the patient being assessed.

4.7 Weight-based medication dosing is the standard for most pediatric medications. Any medication administered to a pediatric patient by agency staff shall be dose-verified against weight-based dosing calculations, confirmed against the physician order, and double-checked by a second qualified clinician or through the agency's dose verification protocol before administration.

4.8 Family and caregiver engagement is not optional in pediatric home health — it is a clinical necessity. The parent or legal guardian is the primary decision-maker, the primary care provider between visits, and the primary implementer of the care plan. Every visit shall include structured caregiver training, and the plan of care shall reflect the caregiver's competency level and training progress.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Pediatric Referral Acceptance and Staffing Determination

6.2 Pediatric SOC Assessment

6.3 Pediatric Plan of Care Development

6.4 Pediatric Visit Execution and Caregiver Training

6.5 Pediatric Mandatory Reporting

6.6 Pediatric Weight-Based Medication Verification

6.7 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors and CDPH surveyors conducting pediatric home health surveys will specifically verify:

1. Clinician pediatric competency. Surveyors will request personnel files for all clinicians assigned to pediatric patients and verify documented pediatric clinical experience and competency validation. Assignment of clinicians without documented pediatric competency is a deficiency.

2. Age-appropriate assessment documentation. Surveyors will examine whether visit notes use adult-oriented templates applied to pediatric patients — this is a frequent deficiency. Assessment findings must be interpreted using age-appropriate normal ranges, and developmental surveillance must be documented.

3. Family-centered care documentation. Surveyors will verify that caregiver training is documented at each visit and that caregiver competency is assessed and tracked. Visit notes that do not document structured caregiver training suggest the service is not being delivered as a skilled home health service.

4. Mandatory reporting compliance. Surveyors may review the agency's training records for mandatory reporting obligations and may inquire about any suspected abuse or neglect situations managed during the survey period.

5. Informed consent from the legal guardian. Surveyors will verify that consent was obtained from the parent or legal guardian, not the minor patient (except for emancipated minors).

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 State Requirements

9.3 Clinical Practice Standards

9.4 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All clinicians assigned to pediatric patients shall receive competency-validated pediatric-specific training before their first independent pediatric patient visit, covering: age-appropriate vital sign reference ranges and assessment technique; developmental milestone surveillance; family-centered care principles; caregiver training documentation; pediatric weight-based medication dosing and verification; mandatory reporting obligations and reporting thresholds; age-appropriate communication techniques; and pediatric-specific clinical documentation standards.

10.2 Pediatric competency shall be validated through: (a) written knowledge assessment (≥80% passing score) covering pediatric vital sign ranges, developmental milestones, and mandatory reporting requirements; (b) clinical skills demonstration or simulation for pediatric assessment technique.

10.3 Annual refresher training on mandatory reporting obligations shall be provided to all clinical staff per HR-ER-009 regardless of whether they currently serve pediatric patients.

10.4 All personnel within scope shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix C) within 14 calendar days of the effective date, any revision, or hire.

11. Version Control

11.1 This policy is maintained per EN-LC-001. Only the most current approved version is valid. Superseded versions shall be archived as "SUPERSEDED — NOT FOR USE."

11.2 Substantive revisions require Governing Body approval documented in meeting minutes, re-acknowledgment by all personnel within scope within 14 calendar days, and policy index update per EN-TG-001. Note: Given the biennial review cycle for this policy, triggered reviews shall occur any time California mandatory reporting law is amended, CMS pediatric home health guidance is updated, or a pediatric adverse event occurs at the agency.

11.3 Non-substantive revisions may be approved by the Administrator with Governing Body notification at the next regular meeting.

CL-SD-022 — Behavioral Health Screening & Referral

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy mandates behavioral health screening at Start of Care and as indicated throughout the home health episode, with defined referral pathways and documentation requirements, for all patients served by Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. Behavioral health conditions — primarily depression, anxiety, and cognitive impairment — are among the most prevalent co-morbidities in the Medicare home health population, yet they are among the most frequently under-identified and under-addressed. The intersection of behavioral health and medical conditions in home health is profound: depression predicts medication non-adherence, slows functional recovery, increases fall risk, drives hospital readmission, and degrades quality of life. Anxiety worsens dyspnea in respiratory patients, elevates blood pressure in cardiac patients, and interferes with engagement in physical therapy. Cognitive impairment undermines the patient's ability to self-manage medications, recognize clinical deterioration, or follow safety instructions — creating compounding risk across every aspect of care. The OASIS assessment explicitly requires behavioral health screening using validated instruments — the PHQ-2/PHQ-9 for depression screening, the BIMS (Brief Interview for Mental Status) for cognitive assessment, and other standardized tools — and the accuracy of these OASIS items directly affects PDGM payment, quality measure performance, and star ratings. Incomplete or inaccurate behavioral health screening is simultaneously a clinical quality failure, a patient safety failure, and an OASIS accuracy failure. This policy ensures that every patient receives systematic, validated behavioral health screening at admission and as indicated, that clinical findings trigger appropriate and documented responses, and that patients with behavioral health needs are connected to appropriate resources through defined referral pathways.

3. Scope

This policy applies to:

All registered nurses (RNs) responsible for conducting OASIS assessments and behavioral health screening at SOC and applicable time points

All licensed vocational nurses (LVNs) who observe and report behavioral health indicators between RN assessment visits

All clinical disciplines who interact with patients and may identify behavioral health concerns

The Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager

Medical Social Workers who serve as primary community resource coordinators for behavioral health referrals

Revenue Cycle staff relying on OASIS behavioral health items for PDGM payment accuracy

4. Policy Statement

4.1 Behavioral health screening shall be conducted at SOC, at each OASIS assessment time point, and as indicated during the episode whenever a change in the patient's behavioral health status is identified by any clinical discipline. Behavioral health screening is not optional — it is a mandatory component of the comprehensive assessment and of OASIS completion.

4.2 The following validated behavioral health screening tools shall be used at the designated time points: (a) PHQ-2 (Patient Health Questionnaire — 2 item) as an initial depression screening tool at SOC, ROC, and recertification — if PHQ-2 score ≥3, proceed to PHQ-9; (b) PHQ-9 (Patient Health Questionnaire — 9 item) as a comprehensive depression assessment when PHQ-2 is positive or when clinical judgment indicates depression; (c) BIMS (Brief Interview for Mental Status) to assess cognitive function — consisting of the repetition of three words, temporal orientation (month, day, year), and recall — scored 0–15 with scores interpreted per CMS OASIS-E2 Guidance Manual; (d) MMSE or MoCA (Mini-Mental State Examination or Montreal Cognitive Assessment) as supplementary cognitive assessment tools when clinically indicated and within the RN's scope; (e) GAD-7 (Generalized Anxiety Disorder — 7 item) as an anxiety screening tool when clinical presentation suggests anxiety; (f) DAST-10 (Drug Abuse Screening Test) or AUDIT-C (Alcohol Use Disorders Identification Test — Consumption) when substance use disorder is a clinical concern.

4.3 The OASIS behavioral health items — including M1700 (Cognitive Function), M1710 (When Confused), M1720 (When Anxious), M1730 (Depressive Feelings), M1740 (Behaviors Demonstrated at Least Once a Week), M1745 (Frequency of Disruptive/Problematic Behaviors), M1750 (Current Level of Clinical Depression), and associated items — shall be completed accurately, using the validated tools specified in Section 4.2, and documented in a manner that is consistent with and supported by the narrative assessment documentation in the visit note per CL-OA-007.

4.4 Every patient who screens positive for depression (PHQ-2 ≥3 or PHQ-9 ≥5), anxiety (GAD-7 ≥5), cognitive impairment (BIMS score <13), or who demonstrates behavioral concerns shall receive: (a) notification of the physician within 24 hours; (b) a referral to the Medical Social Worker for psychosocial assessment per CL-SD-005; (c) documentation in the plan of care of the behavioral health finding and the planned response; (d) follow-up at each subsequent visit to monitor behavioral health status.

4.5 Safety screening shall be conducted as part of the behavioral health assessment for any patient who screens positive for depression. All patients with a PHQ-9 score ≥10, or who endorse item 9 of the PHQ-9 ("thoughts that you would be better off dead or hurting yourself"), shall be assessed for suicidality. Suicidality assessment findings shall be reported to the physician immediately, and a safety plan shall be established per Section 6.5.

4.6 Referrals for behavioral health services shall be made with the patient's and caregiver's informed understanding and consent. The clinician shall explain the reason for the referral, the type of behavioral health services being recommended, and how those services will support the patient's overall recovery. Referrals shall not be made in a manner that stigmatizes or dismisses the patient's emotional experience.

4.7 All behavioral health screening results, physician notifications, referrals, and follow-up findings shall be documented in the patient's clinical record with sufficient specificity to demonstrate that the screening was performed using a validated tool, that results were accurately recorded, and that findings triggered appropriate clinical responses.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Behavioral Health Screening at SOC

6.2 Ongoing Behavioral Health Monitoring During the Episode

6.3 Physician Notification and Plan of Care Integration

6.4 Behavioral Health Referral Pathways

6.5 Suicidality Assessment and Safety Planning

6.6 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors will verify that behavioral health screening is conducted using validated tools and that OASIS behavioral health items are accurate. They will: (a) select patients with known behavioral health diagnoses and verify that appropriate screening tools were used; (b) compare OASIS behavioral health item responses to screening tool scores documented in the clinical record; (c) verify that positive screens resulted in physician notification and plan of care integration; (d) ask clinicians to describe the PHQ-2 and BIMS administration protocol. OASIS validation auditors will specifically target behavioral health items as they directly affect PDGM payment classification.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 CMS Guidance

9.3 Clinical Practice Standards

9.4 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All RNs shall receive competency-validated training on behavioral health screening within 14 calendar days of hire and annually thereafter, covering: PHQ-2 and PHQ-9 administration, scoring, and clinical interpretation; BIMS administration and scoring per CMS OASIS-E2 guidance; GAD-7 administration and scoring; suicidality assessment using the C-SSRS; safety plan development; behavioral health referral pathways; OASIS behavioral health item coding; and documentation standards.

10.2 BIMS administration competency shall be validated by observation — the RN must demonstrate correct administration of all three BIMS components before independently conducting OASIS assessments. This shall be assessed at hire and annually.

10.3 All clinical disciplines shall receive training on behavioral health observation and reporting responsibilities — specifically, how to identify and report behavioral health changes between RN assessment visits.

10.4 All personnel within scope shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix B) within 14 calendar days of the effective date, revision, or hire.

11. Version Control

11.1 This policy is maintained per EN-LC-001. Only the most current approved version is valid. 11.2 Substantive revisions require Governing Body approval. 11.3 Non-substantive revisions approved by Administrator.

CL-SD-023 — Palliative & End-of-Life Care

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy establishes the standards for palliative care delivery, advance directive discussions, goals-of-care conversations, and hospice referral coordination at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. The Medicare home health benefit is a curative benefit — it requires that patients be receiving skilled services directed toward recovery, functional improvement, or management of a condition. However, many patients receiving home health services have advanced chronic illnesses, life-limiting diagnoses, or declining functional trajectories that place them at the intersection of curative and palliative care. Palliative care is not synonymous with hospice — it is an approach to care that can and should be integrated alongside curative treatment at any stage of serious illness, focusing on relief of suffering (pain, dyspnea, nausea, anxiety, existential distress), support for the patient's and family's goals, and optimization of quality of life. The home health clinician is often the first healthcare professional to recognize that a patient's trajectory is declining, that the patient has unmet palliative care needs, or that the patient and family have not had an informed conversation about their goals and options. The clinical, ethical, and legal obligations that arise at this juncture — around advance directives, goals-of-care conversations, CPR preferences, and the transition to hospice — require that every clinician at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. has the knowledge, skill, and clear procedural guidance to navigate them with competence, compassion, and legal precision. This policy implements 42 CFR § 484.60 (plan of care conformance), CL-PR-002 (Advance Directive Compliance), and the clinical standards of the National Consensus Project for Quality Palliative Care Clinical Practice Guidelines.

3. Scope

This policy applies to:

All registered nurses (RNs) providing skilled nursing to patients with serious, advanced, or life-limiting illness

All licensed vocational nurses (LVNs) who may encounter patients with palliative care needs

All clinical disciplines (PT, OT, SLP, MSW) whose services intersect with palliative care goals

Medical Social Workers — the primary coordinator of psychosocial palliative care support and hospice referral

The Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager

All clinical disciplines who interact with patients approaching end of life

4. Policy Statement

4.1 Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. is committed to delivering palliative care principles — symptom management, goals-of-care alignment, psychosocial and spiritual support, and family engagement — integrated into the plan of care for every patient with a serious illness, without waiting for a prognosis of terminal illness or a decision to forgo curative treatment.

4.2 The advance directive status of every patient shall be assessed, documented, and honored as a mandatory component of the SOC assessment per CL-PR-002. Advance directive documentation shall be accessible to all treating clinicians at every visit.

4.3 The assigned RN and MSW shall engage in ongoing, sensitive, and patient-directed goals-of-care conversations throughout the episode, recognizing the patient's right to understand their prognosis, their treatment options, and the choices available to them — including the option of transitioning to hospice care.

4.4 Patients with progressive, life-limiting illnesses whose clinical trajectory suggests declining functional status or who repeatedly experience hospitalizations for the same underlying condition shall be proactively assessed for their palliative care needs, and a discussion of goals of care — including hospice eligibility — shall be initiated with the patient and caregiver in collaboration with the attending physician.

4.5 Hospice referral shall be initiated when: (a) the physician believes the patient has a life expectancy of 6 months or less if the illness runs its normal course; (b) the patient is no longer interested in curative or life-prolonging treatment; (c) the patient's clinical trajectory suggests that the goals of home health care (functional improvement, recovery) are no longer achievable; or (d) the patient and family independently request information about hospice. Hospice referral shall be coordinated per Section 6.5 and CL-CP-007 (Transfer & Referral Procedures).

4.6 Symptoms commonly associated with serious illness and end of life — including pain, dyspnea, nausea, constipation (especially opioid-induced), anxiety, agitation, oral secretions, and delirium — shall be managed with the same clinical rigor applied to any other clinical condition, using physician-ordered pharmacologic and non-pharmacologic interventions, with documented assessment, intervention, and reassessment at every applicable visit.

4.7 Spiritual and cultural needs at end of life shall be assessed and addressed as part of the plan of care. Clinicians shall not impose personal beliefs on patients or families, shall seek to understand the patient's and family's cultural and spiritual framework for illness and death, and shall coordinate chaplaincy or spiritual care resources when indicated.

4.8 Family and caregiver support during serious illness and after patient death is a recognized component of palliative care. MSW and nursing shall assess caregiver burden, grief, and bereavement needs, and shall coordinate appropriate support resources.

4.9 No patient shall be referred to hospice without their informed consent and the consent of their legal representative where applicable. The hospice election is the patient's choice — the agency's role is to ensure the patient and family have the information they need to make an informed decision.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Advance Directive Assessment at SOC

6.2 Ongoing Palliative Care Assessment and Integration

6.3 Goals-of-Care Conversations

6.4 Symptom Management at End of Life

6.5 Hospice Referral Coordination

6.6 Bereavement Support

6.7 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors will verify that advance directives are assessed, documented, and accessible in the clinical record. They will ask clinical staff whether they know how to access the advance directive for any patient they are caring for. Surveyors will also examine whether the plan of care reflects the patient's expressed goals — a plan of care focused on aggressive functional restoration for a patient who has expressed comfort-only goals is an alignment failure. ADR auditors will examine whether palliative care visits are documented with sufficient clinical specificity to support skilled nursing — a visit note that reads "patient comfortable" without symptom assessment or documented interventions does not demonstrate skilled care.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 State Requirements

9.3 Clinical Practice Standards

9.4 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All RNs and MSWs shall receive competency-validated training on palliative and end-of-life care within 14 calendar days of hire and annually thereafter, covering: palliative care principles and the distinction between palliative care and hospice; advance directive types, legal requirements under California law, and POLST execution; goals-of-care communication skills using an evidence-based framework (REMAP, SPIKES, or equivalent); end-of-life symptom assessment and management (pain, dyspnea, delirium, constipation, secretions); opioid use for dyspnea in comfort-focused patients; hospice referral coordination per CL-CP-007; bereavement support; and documentation standards for palliative care visits demonstrating skilled nursing.

10.2 All clinical disciplines shall receive training on advance directive status awareness — they must be able to identify whether a patient has an advance directive and know where to find it in the clinical record.

10.3 All personnel within scope shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix B) within 14 calendar days of the effective date, revision, or hire.

11. Version Control

11.1 Maintained per EN-LC-001. Only the most current approved version is valid. 11.2 Substantive revisions require Governing Body approval. 11.3 Non-substantive revisions approved by Administrator.

CL-SD-024 — Missed Visit & Rescheduling

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy establishes the protocols for managing, documenting, and reporting missed visits and rescheduling requirements at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. A missed visit is any scheduled patient visit that does not occur as planned — regardless of whether it was missed due to patient refusal, patient unavailability, clinician absence, scheduling error, or emergency. Missed visits are significant clinical events: they represent a gap in skilled care delivery, potentially interrupt therapeutic treatment sequences, increase the risk of clinical deterioration going undetected, and — when not properly documented and managed — create documentation integrity failures, LUPA risk, and False Claims Act exposure if the ordered visit frequency is not delivered but a full episode payment is collected. The CMS Home Health Compare quality measure "Timely Initiation of Care" and the PDGM LUPA threshold both create direct financial and quality consequences for visits that are not delivered as ordered. This policy ensures that every missed visit is identified on the same day it occurs, is documented with the reason and the clinical assessment of patient impact, is rescheduled within the shortest clinically appropriate timeframe, is communicated to the physician when clinically significant, and is tracked through the QAPI program as a quality indicator.

3. Scope

This policy applies to:

All clinical disciplines (RN, LVN, PT, OT, SLP, MSW, HHA) who experience or identify missed patient visits

Clinical Coordinators responsible for managing visit scheduling and rescheduling

The Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager responsible for clinical oversight and missed visit response

Revenue Cycle staff monitoring LUPA risk and visit frequency compliance

The QAPI Coordinator tracking missed visit rates as a quality indicator

This policy applies to all types of missed visits for all disciplines during any phase of the home health episode.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 Every missed visit shall be identified on the day it was scheduled and shall be documented in the patient's clinical record on the same day with: (a) the type of visit missed (discipline, visit number in the episode); (b) the reason for the missed visit; (c) the responsible party for the missed visit (patient, caregiver, clinician, scheduling error, emergency, weather, other); (d) the clinical assessment of patient impact — what clinical risk does the missed visit create for this specific patient?; (e) the rescheduling plan.

4.2 Every missed visit shall be rescheduled at the earliest clinically appropriate opportunity. Rescheduling does not mean adding a visit to the end of the certification period — it means providing the missed visit as soon as the patient is available and clinically appropriate to do so. The goal is minimal disruption to the patient's therapeutic trajectory.

4.3 The reason for every missed visit shall be classified using the agency's standard missed visit reason taxonomy (Appendix A) to enable accurate QAPI tracking and identification of systemic causes.

4.4 The physician shall be notified within 24 hours of a missed visit when: (a) the patient refused care and the refusal creates a patient safety concern; (b) the missed visit disrupts a critical clinical sequence (e.g., missed wound care, missed insulin administration, missed critical medication monitoring); (c) more than 2 consecutive visits in any discipline have been missed; (d) the patient was not available at home and was not reachable by telephone — raising a potential welfare concern.

4.5 If a patient cannot be reached by telephone and was not found at home for a scheduled visit, the clinical coordinator or assigned clinician shall attempt contact using all known telephone numbers and emergency contacts within 4 hours of the missed visit. If the patient is still unreachable after 4 hours, notify the Director of Nursing immediately. The Director of Nursing shall assess whether a welfare check is warranted and shall coordinate with the physician and, if necessary, emergency services.

4.6 Patient refusal of a visit shall be documented with specificity: the refusal shall be documented in the patient's clinical record with the patient's specific stated reason (not "patient refused"), any education or counseling provided regarding the clinical implications of the refusal, and the plan for the next visit.

4.7 Repeated missed visits — defined as 3 or more missed visits in any discipline within a 30-day period for the same patient — shall trigger a clinical case review by the Director of Nursing to assess: the patient's engagement and adherence barriers; whether the plan of care is appropriate and achievable; whether non-compliance discharge criteria per CL-CP-006 Section 6.5 may be applicable; and what interventions can improve care delivery.

4.8 All missed visits shall be tracked in the agency's QAPI dashboard per QA-PI-002 as a quality indicator. The Director of Nursing shall report missed visit rates, reasons, and trends to the QAPI Committee quarterly. A missed visit rate exceeding agency-defined threshold shall trigger a Performance Improvement Project per QA-PI-001.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Same-Day Identification and Documentation

6.2 Rescheduling Protocol

6.3 Patient Not Found / Welfare Check Protocol

6.4 Patient Refusal Documentation

6.5 Repeated Missed Visit Clinical Case Review

6.6 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors will verify that missed visits are documented as such — they will review visit schedules and identify any ordered visits for which no visit note exists, and they will check whether a Missed Visit Record was completed. Surveyors will also verify that patient refusals are documented with specific reasons rather than the generic notation "patient refused." ADR auditors will cross-reference ordered visit frequency against delivered visit documentation — undelivered ordered visits that appear as billable in claims are False Claims Act risk items.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 CMS Guidance

9.3 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All clinical disciplines and Clinical Coordinators shall receive training on missed visit documentation and management within 14 calendar days of hire and annually thereafter, covering: the same-day documentation requirement; the Missed Visit Reason Taxonomy; the patient refusal documentation standard; the patient not-found welfare check protocol; the physician notification trigger criteria; and LUPA risk implications of missed visits.

10.2 All personnel within scope shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix B) within 14 calendar days of the effective date, revision, or hire.

CL-SD-025 — Ordered Visit Frequency Compliance & Monitoring

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy requires systematic monitoring of actual visit delivery against physician-ordered frequency and disciplines, with defined variance thresholds, documentation requirements, and corrective action triggers at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. This policy exists because there is an inherent tension in home health between the ideal (every ordered visit delivered exactly as ordered) and the reality (patient refusals, missed visits, scheduling constraints, clinician availability, and patient clinical variability). If the tension is left unmanaged, the result is a systematic pattern of under-delivery of ordered services that simultaneously constitutes a patient safety failure (the patient receives less care than the physician determined was clinically necessary), a documentation compliance failure (the clinical record does not reflect a defensible explanation for the variance), a billing compliance failure (a full PDGM episode payment is collected for a certification period in which the ordered services were not delivered), and a False Claims Act risk. The PDGM Low Utilization Payment Adjustment (LUPA) exists precisely because CMS recognizes that low-visit episodes should be paid differently — when a patient is billed at the full PDGM rate for an episode but receives fewer visits than the physician ordered without clinical justification, the agency has potentially submitted a false claim. This policy ensures that visit delivery is monitored in real time, that variances between ordered and delivered frequency are identified immediately, that every variance is documented with a clinical explanation, that physician-ordered frequency is the floor — not the ceiling — of what the patient should receive, and that systemic under-delivery is detected and corrected before it becomes a financial and legal compliance failure.

3. Scope

This policy applies to:

All registered nurses (RNs) managing active patient caseloads

All clinical disciplines (PT, OT, SLP, MSW, HHA) delivering patient visits

Clinical Coordinators managing visit scheduling and monitoring

The Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager responsible for visit delivery oversight

Revenue Cycle staff monitoring LUPA risk and episode billing

The QAPI Coordinator tracking visit utilization as a quality indicator

4. Policy Statement

4.1 The physician-ordered visit frequency, specified in the physician-approved plan of care, represents the minimum standard of service delivery for each patient during each certification period. Every ordered visit shall be delivered unless a specific, documented clinical or patient-initiated reason justifies a variance, and that reason shall be communicated to the physician when it creates a clinically significant deviation from the ordered plan.

4.2 The agency shall maintain a centralized Visit Frequency Monitoring System that tracks, for each active patient and each discipline, the ordered visit frequency, the delivered visit count, and the variance — updated daily and reviewed by Clinical Coordinators at minimum daily and by the Director of Nursing at minimum weekly.

4.3 Variance thresholds shall be defined as follows: (a) Tier 1 Variance — 1 visit below the ordered frequency within a 2-week period: Clinical Coordinator documents the reason and schedules a makeup visit. Director of Nursing notified if the reason is patient refusal or clinician unavailability. (b) Tier 2 Variance — 2 or more visits below the ordered frequency within a 2-week period, OR any variance that creates LUPA risk: Director of Nursing review required within 24 hours. Physician notification within 24 hours. Plan of care modification assessment per CL-CP-002. (c) Tier 3 Variance — Pattern of under-delivery across 2 or more consecutive 2-week periods, OR variance creating a LUPA for the episode: Director of Nursing and Administrator review. Physician notification and plan of care modification required. Compliance Officer notification if billing has been submitted. QAPI Performance Improvement Project initiated per QA-PI-001.

4.4 Every variance between ordered and delivered visit frequency shall be documented with a clinical explanation that addresses: (a) why the visit was not delivered; (b) the clinical impact of the variance on the patient; (c) what actions were taken to minimize the impact; (d) the physician notification status.

4.5 If the physician-ordered visit frequency is consistently undeliverable because the patient refuses the number of visits ordered, the appropriate clinical action is a plan of care modification to reflect the frequency the patient will actually accept — not continued ordering of visits that are never delivered. Ordering visits that the patient consistently refuses and never delivers, while collecting full episode payment, constitutes billing for services not rendered.

4.6 The agency shall never provide more visits than ordered without a physician order authorizing additional visits. Providing unordered visits is also a compliance violation — both under-delivery and over-delivery of ordered services without physician authorization are non-compliant.

4.7 Visit frequency compliance shall be a standing agenda item at each QAPI Committee meeting per QA-PG-003, with discipline-specific delivery rates, LUPA rates, and corrective actions reported.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Visit Frequency Monitoring System — Daily Management

6.2 Tier 1 Variance Response

6.3 Tier 2 Variance Response

6.4 Tier 3 Variance Response

6.5 Plan of Care Frequency Modification Process

6.6 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors will compare ordered visit frequencies in the plan of care against delivered visit counts in the clinical record. Surveyors will flag episodes where fewer visits were delivered than ordered without documented clinical justification. ADR auditors and MAC reviewers will specifically target episodes where the delivered visit count is below the LUPA threshold for the patient's payment group — these are treated as potential billing errors or False Claims Act concerns. OIG audits of home health agencies routinely identify patterns of claiming full PDGM payment for episodes in which ordered visits were systematically not delivered.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 CMS Guidance

9.3 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All Clinical Coordinators shall receive training on the Visit Frequency Monitoring System at hire and whenever the system is updated, covering: daily report generation; variance classification using the three-tier system; rescheduling timelines; LUPA risk identification; and escalation protocol.

10.2 All RNs and clinical discipline leads shall receive training on ordered visit frequency compliance within 14 calendar days of hire and annually thereafter, covering: the clinical and billing implications of under-delivery; the physician notification requirement for Tier 2 variances; the plan of care modification process; and the documentation requirements for variances.

10.3 Revenue Cycle staff shall receive training on LUPA threshold identification and billing level compliance at hire and annually.

10.4 All personnel within scope shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix A) within 14 calendar days of the effective date, revision, or hire.

11. Version Control

11.1 This policy is maintained under the agency's enterprise policy lifecycle management system per policy EN-LC-001. 11.2 Only the most current approved version is valid. Superseded versions shall be archived as "SUPERSEDED — NOT FOR USE." 11.3 Any substantive revision requires: (a) review and approval by the Governing Body, documented in meeting minutes; (b) re-acknowledgment by all personnel within scope within 14 calendar days of the revised effective date; (c) update to the enterprise policy index per EN-TG-001. 11.4 Non-substantive revisions may be approved by the Administrator with Governing Body notification at the next regular meeting.

CL-CA-001 — Patient Assessment — Comprehensive

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy mandates completion of a comprehensive patient assessment — including all required OASIS data elements at applicable time points — for every patient admitted to home health services at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. The comprehensive assessment is the clinical foundation upon which every other clinical activity in the home health episode depends. It is simultaneously: the clinical document that establishes the patient's eligibility for the Medicare home health benefit (homebound status, need for skilled services); the data source for all OASIS items that drive PDGM payment classification; the basis for the individualized plan of care; the baseline against which all clinical progress is measured; and the primary document that CMS surveyors and ADR auditors examine when evaluating whether the patient legitimately required — and received — skilled home health services. An inadequate, incomplete, or inaccurate comprehensive assessment cascades failure across the entire episode: an inaccurate OASIS produces the wrong PDGM payment group; an incomplete clinical picture produces a plan of care that does not reflect the patient's actual needs; missing homebound status documentation creates billing vulnerability; and inadequate assessment documentation fails to justify the skilled services billed. This policy implements 42 CFR § 484.55 — Condition of Participation: Comprehensive Assessment of Patients — which requires that a registered nurse complete a comprehensive assessment for each patient admitted to home health care that accurately reflects the patient's current health status and includes information that may be used to inform the individualized plan of care.

3. Scope

This policy applies to:

All registered nurses (RNs) authorized to conduct comprehensive assessments at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. — only RNs may conduct comprehensive assessments; LVNs, therapists, and other disciplines may not independently conduct the comprehensive assessment or complete OASIS assessments

The Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager responsible for assessment quality oversight

All clinical disciplines whose initial discipline-specific evaluations contribute to but do not substitute for the comprehensive assessment

Medical Records and OASIS submission staff

Revenue Cycle staff relying on OASIS data for payment classification

This policy governs comprehensive assessments at all applicable time points: Start of Care (SOC), Resumption of Care (ROC), Recertification (RECERT), Follow-Up (FU), Discharge (DC), and Transfer (TRN). Discipline-specific assessments (PT evaluation, OT evaluation, SLP evaluation, MSW psychosocial assessment) are supplemental to the comprehensive assessment and are governed by the respective discipline-specific policies (CL-SD-002 through CL-SD-005). OASIS-specific data collection requirements, accuracy standards, and submission requirements are governed by CL-OA-001 through CL-OA-019, which cross-reference this policy as the clinical basis for OASIS data.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 A comprehensive patient assessment shall be completed by a qualified registered nurse at the Start of Care visit and at all subsequent applicable OASIS time points. The comprehensive assessment shall begin at the time of the first billable visit — the SOC visit — and shall be completed at the patient's home.

4.2 Only a registered nurse currently licensed by the California Board of Registered Nursing and meeting the assessment competency requirements of this policy may complete a comprehensive assessment for home health patients. Licensed vocational nurses, therapists, and other clinical disciplines shall not complete comprehensive assessments or OASIS assessments independently, regardless of the clinical services they provide.

4.3 The comprehensive assessment shall be completed within 5 calendar days following the start of the care episode — meaning the SOC assessment shall be completed at or within 5 calendar days of the first billable visit per 42 CFR § 484.55(a)(1). The 5-day window applies to the completion and locking of the assessment in the EHR, not merely to the collection of data during the visit.

4.4 The comprehensive assessment shall include all domains specified in Section 6.2 and shall incorporate all applicable OASIS data elements as defined in the current CMS OASIS-E2 guidance. The assessment shall be individualized and patient-specific — template-driven assessments that do not reflect the patient's actual clinical presentation at the time of the assessment are not compliant with 42 CFR § 484.55.

4.5 The comprehensive assessment shall include a determination of homebound status per CL-CA-005, clearly documented with patient-specific functional limitations that support the homebound determination.

4.6 The comprehensive assessment shall include a determination of the patient's need for skilled services — specifically identifying which skilled disciplines are required and why they are medically necessary for the treatment of this patient's conditions.

4.7 The comprehensive assessment findings shall form the direct basis of the plan of care. Every element of the plan of care — diagnoses, services, frequency, goals, medications, treatments, and safety measures — shall be traceable to findings documented in the comprehensive assessment.

4.8 The assigned RN shall incorporate or coordinate with the findings of all discipline-specific evaluations (PT, OT, SLP, MSW) conducted at or near the SOC into the unified plan of care, ensuring that the plan is a truly interdisciplinary document rather than a series of parallel, disconnected discipline plans.

4.9 All OASIS items shall be completed accurately, using the current CMS OASIS-E2 Guidance Manual for all response selection decisions. The RN completing the assessment is solely responsible for the accuracy of the OASIS coding — OASIS responses shall not be altered by non-clinical staff, revenue cycle personnel, or billing coders.

4.10 A comprehensive re-assessment shall be conducted at each OASIS time point (ROC, RECERT, FU as applicable, TRN, DC). The re-assessment shall reflect the patient's current status at the time of the re-assessment — not the status at the prior assessment or the status at an earlier time point. Each OASIS assessment is a contemporaneous point-in-time assessment of the patient's current condition per CL-OA-009.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Pre-Assessment Preparation

6.2 Comprehensive Assessment — Required Domains and Content

6.3 OASIS Data Collection and Completion

6.4 Assessment-to-Plan of Care Integration

6.5 Recertification and Subsequent Assessments

6.6 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors conducting a standard survey under SOM Appendix B will specifically verify under Tag G140 (42 CFR § 484.55):

1. Evidence that a registered nurse completed the comprehensive assessment. Surveyors will verify that the OASIS assessment was completed by an RN. Assessment by LVNs, therapists, or non-clinical staff is a Condition-level deficiency.

2. Evidence that the assessment was completed within 5 calendar days. Surveyors will compare the OASIS lock date to the SOC date. Late assessments are a documentation compliance deficiency.

3. Evidence that the assessment is comprehensive and individualized. Surveyors will review OASIS assessments for template-driven responses that do not reflect the specific patient's status. Cookie-cutter assessments are cited as failures to meet the comprehensive assessment standard.

4. Evidence that OASIS items are accurate. OASIS validation auditors will select specific items — particularly functional status items (GG section), wound items (M1311–M1350), behavioral health items (M1700–M1750), and medication management items — and compare the coded response to the narrative assessment documentation. Inconsistencies are cited as OASIS inaccuracy deficiencies.

5. Evidence that the assessment led to an individualized plan of care. Surveyors will compare the plan of care goals and services to the assessment findings. A plan of care that does not reflect the assessment is evidence that the assessment was not the basis for the plan.

6. Evidence of homebound status. Surveyors will specifically review the homebound documentation in the assessment for patient-specific, clinical language describing why the patient cannot leave home without considerable effort. Generic homebound language ("patient has difficulty ambulating") without patient-specific limitations is frequently cited.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 CMS Guidance

9.3 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All RNs shall complete the Comprehensive Assessment Competency Program (Appendix A) before independently conducting comprehensive assessments. The program shall include: (a) didactic content covering all assessment domains in Section 6.2; (b) OASIS item-specific training per the CMS OASIS-E2 Guidance Manual; (c) standardized tool administration (PHQ-2/PHQ-9, BIMS, MAHC-10 as applicable) with validated competency demonstration; (d) homebound status documentation standards; (e) skilled service need documentation standards; (f) pre-submission quality review process; (g) assessment-to-plan of care integration; (h) documentation standards demonstrating individualized, patient-specific assessment content.

10.2 The Director of Nursing or a designated preceptor RN shall directly supervise and evaluate the first independent SOC assessment of every newly hired or newly authorized RN before adding the RN to the Authorized Assessor Roster.

10.3 Annual refresher competency for comprehensive assessment and OASIS accuracy shall be completed by all authorized RN assessors, incorporating any changes to the OASIS data set or CMS guidance issued since the prior annual training.

10.4 All personnel within scope shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix B) within 14 calendar days of the effective date, any revision, or hire/appointment.

10.5 The Director of Nursing shall maintain the Comprehensive Assessment Competency Program training records and the Authorized Assessor Roster for all RNs and shall report any non-compliance to the Administrator within 7 calendar days of any training deadline.

11. Version Control

11.1 This policy is maintained under the agency's enterprise policy lifecycle management system per policy EN-LC-001. 11.2 Only the most current approved version is valid. Superseded versions shall be archived as "SUPERSEDED — NOT FOR USE." 11.3 Any substantive revision requires: (a) review and approval by the Governing Body, documented in meeting minutes; (b) re-acknowledgment by all personnel within scope within 14 calendar days of the revised effective date; (c) update to the enterprise policy index per EN-TG-001. 11.4 Non-substantive revisions may be approved by the Administrator with Governing Body notification at the next regular meeting.
