CL-PR-002 — Advance Directive Compliance

1. Policy Header

Field	Value

Policy ID	CL-PR-002

Policy Title	Advance Directive Compliance

Domain	CL — Clinical Operations

Subdomain	PR — Patient Rights & Safety

Classification Tier	REQUIRED

Status	ACTIVE

Review Cycle	Annual

Access Tier	Tier 2 — Restricted

Policy Owner / Steward	Director of Nursing

Effective Date	2025-07-10

Version	1.0

Approved By	Governing Body Chair — Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc.

Last Reviewed	2025-07-10

Next Review Date	2026-07-10

Supersedes	N/A (Initial Version)

2. Purpose

This policy defines the requirements for identifying, documenting, and honoring patient advance directives per federal and state law at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. An advance directive is a patient's legally binding expression of their wishes about medical treatment in future circumstances when they may not be able to communicate those wishes directly. It is the clearest and most unambiguous form of patient autonomy — a patient's voice preserved in a document, speaking forward through time to guide the people who will care for them when they cannot speak for themselves. Honoring it is not optional. Failing to honor it is a legal violation, an ethical failure, and a profound betrayal of the patient's trust.

The legal framework governing advance directives in California is both robust and nuanced. California's Health Care Decisions Law (Probate Code § 4600 et seq.) governs the validity, interpretation, and required honoring of advance directives — including Living Wills, Durable Powers of Attorney for Health Care (DPAHC), and Physician Orders for Life-Sustaining Treatment (POLST). Federal law (42 CFR § 484.50) requires that Medicare-certified home health agencies document advance directive status, communicate relevant agency policies about advance directives, and ensure that clinicians do not override a patient's advance directive based on personal beliefs.

The home health context creates a specific and critical responsibility: when a patient deteriorates, falls, or stops breathing in their home, the response of the agency's clinician in that moment — or the response of emergency services called by the patient's caregiver — determines whether the patient's advance directives are honored or whether the patient receives interventions they explicitly chose not to have. A clinician who does not know the patient's advance directive status, or who cannot locate the POLST in an emergency, cannot honor it. This policy ensures that advance directive information is known, accessible, accurately documented, and honored at every stage of the patient's home health episode.

3. Scope

This policy applies to:

All registered nurses conducting SOC, ROC, and RECERT assessments at which advance directive status is assessed

All clinical disciplines who may be present at a patient's home during an emergency that implicates advance directives

The Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager

Medical Records staff responsible for advance directive document management

On-call nursing staff who may respond to patient emergencies

All personnel who interact with patients or their legally authorized representatives regarding advance care planning

4. Policy Statement

4.1 Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. shall assess, document, and honor the advance directive status of every patient admitted to home health services. Advance directive status shall be assessed at the SOC visit and shall be reassessed at each recertification and whenever the patient's clinical trajectory or stated wishes change.

4.2 The agency shall provide every patient with written information about the patient's right to make decisions regarding medical care, including the right to accept or refuse treatment and the right to formulate advance directives. This information shall be provided before or at the initiation of home health services.

4.3 The agency shall document in the patient's clinical record whether the patient has executed an advance directive, the type of advance directive, and its content (or the fact that the patient does not have an advance directive).

4.4 The agency shall not condition the provision of services on whether the patient has or has not executed an advance directive. The absence of an advance directive shall not be treated as a reason to withhold information about advance care planning or to provide more aggressive treatment than the patient would choose.

4.5 The agency shall ensure that a copy of the patient's advance directive is available in the patient's clinical record and, for patients with POLST or DNR orders, that the original document is accessible in the patient's home in a location known to the patient, caregiver, and all treating clinicians.

4.6 The agency shall honor the patient's advance directive to the extent possible within the agency's scope of services. When a patient has a DNR or a POLST specifying comfort-only care, the agency shall not initiate CPR on that patient unless the patient has revoked the directive and that revocation has been communicated to the agency. When an advance directive specifies treatment choices that require emergency medical services (e.g., DNR but with treatment for pain), the agency shall communicate those choices to the patient, caregiver, and emergency services responders.

4.7 If there is a conflict between an advance directive and the patient's expressed wishes at the time of a clinical interaction — or a conflict between the advance directive and the caregiver's wishes — the Director of Nursing shall be notified immediately and the conflict shall be addressed through the process defined in Section 6.4.

4.8 Clinicians shall not allow personal moral or religious objections to override a patient's legally valid advance directive. A clinician who has a personal objection to honoring a specific advance directive shall disclose the objection to the Director of Nursing, who shall arrange for a non-objecting clinician to provide care to that patient.

5. Definitions

Term	Definition

Advance Directive	A legal document in which a patient with decision-making capacity expresses their wishes regarding medical treatment in future circumstances when they may be unable to communicate those wishes. Common forms include Living Wills, Durable Powers of Attorney for Healthcare, and POLST.

Living Will	An advance directive in which the patient expresses their wishes about specific types of medical treatment — particularly life-sustaining treatment — in defined circumstances (e.g., terminal illness, persistent vegetative state).

Durable Power of Attorney for Healthcare (DPAHC)	An advance directive in which the patient designates a specific person (the healthcare agent) to make healthcare decisions on the patient's behalf when the patient lacks decision-making capacity.

Healthcare Agent	The person designated by the patient in a DPAHC to make healthcare decisions when the patient lacks capacity. Also called a healthcare proxy, surrogate decision-maker, or patient advocate.

POLST (Physician Orders for Life-Sustaining Treatment)	A portable medical order, signed by a California physician, that translates the patient's goals and preferences for life-sustaining treatment into immediately actionable medical orders. In California, the POLST form addresses CPR preferences, level of medical intervention, and artificial nutrition. It is a physician order, not merely a statement of wishes, and is binding on emergency responders.

DNR (Do Not Resuscitate)	A physician's order directing that CPR shall not be attempted in the event of cardiac or respiratory arrest. In the home setting, a POLST specifying "no CPR" is the standard DNR mechanism for pre-hospital emergency responders.

Decision-Making Capacity	The ability to understand information about a proposed treatment, appreciate the consequences of accepting or refusing the treatment, reason about the options, and communicate a choice. Distinct from legal competency, which is determined by a court.

Revocation	The patient's act of canceling or withdrawing an advance directive. A patient with decision-making capacity may revoke any advance directive at any time, by any means — including verbal revocation — regardless of how the directive was originally made.

Surrogate Decision-Maker	A person authorized under California law to make healthcare decisions on behalf of a patient who lacks decision-making capacity, when no healthcare agent has been designated. California establishes a surrogate priority list: spouse/domestic partner; adult children; parents; adult siblings; etc.

6. Procedures

6.1 Advance Directive Assessment at SOC

Step	Responsible Party	Action	Timeframe

6.1.1	Assigned RN	At the SOC visit, ask the patient directly: "Do you have any documents that express your wishes about medical care if you were unable to speak for yourself — such as a Healthcare Power of Attorney, a Living Will, or a POLST form?" If the patient has designated a healthcare agent, ask: "Who would speak for you if you couldn't speak for yourself, and do you have that in writing?" Document the patient's response.	At the SOC visit.

6.1.2	Assigned RN	If the patient has an advance directive of any type: (a) request to see the document; (b) review it to understand its content — specifically, what decisions has the patient made, and who has been designated as healthcare agent if anyone; (c) document the advance directive type (Living Will, DPAHC, POLST, or combination), the date of execution, the identity of the healthcare agent (if DPAHC), and the key decision provisions (e.g., "POLST: No CPR; Full treatment otherwise"); (d) make a copy of the document for the clinical record; (e) document the location of the original document in the patient's home — this is critical for emergency situations.	At the SOC visit.

6.1.3	Assigned RN	If the patient has a POLST: (a) verify that the POLST is signed by a California physician; (b) verify that the POLST is the current version the patient intends to be in force (POLST forms are not automatically updated — a patient may have multiple POLST forms, only the most recent is operative); (c) ensure the POLST is visible and accessible in the patient's home — the California standard is the refrigerator door or a similarly prominent location known to emergency responders; (d) educate the patient and caregiver about the POLST's effect — specifically, that emergency medical responders will honor the POLST if it is visible to them; (e) verify that the caregiver understands not to call 911 without first reviewing the POLST to know the patient's wishes.	At the SOC visit.

6.1.4	Assigned RN	If the patient does not have an advance directive: (a) provide written information about advance directives and the patient's right to create one; (b) offer to connect the patient with resources for creating an advance directive (the MSW can assist; the agency's patient rights materials include information about local resources); (c) document that the patient was offered information and the patient's response; (d) do not pressure the patient to create an advance directive — the decision is entirely voluntary.	At the SOC visit.

6.1.5	Assigned RN	Document the complete advance directive assessment in the OASIS assessment (M1051 — Advance Directive) consistent with the CMS OASIS-E2 Guidance Manual response options and consistent with the clinical record per CL-OA-007. Ensure the OASIS response accurately reflects the patient's advance directive status.	At the SOC visit; OASIS locked within 5 calendar days.

6.1.6	Medical Records	File a copy of the patient's advance directive document(s) in the clinical record (EHR — Folder 03, Advance Directives). Verify that all treating clinicians are informed of the advance directive status through the EHR's alert or flag system.	Within 5 business days of the SOC.

6.2 Advance Directive Information for All Treating Clinicians

Step	Responsible Party	Action	Timeframe

6.2.1	Director of Nursing	Ensure that the EHR is configured to display a visible alert on the patient's record for any patient with an active advance directive — particularly a POLST specifying no CPR. The alert shall display the advance directive type and key decision provisions.	EHR configuration maintained continuously.

6.2.2	All Clinicians	At the beginning of each visit, verify your awareness of the patient's advance directive status by reviewing the clinical record flag or the EHR alert. If the patient's advance directive status is unknown or unclear, contact the Director of Nursing before proceeding with the visit.	At each visit.

6.2.3	On-Call Nursing	When responding to an after-hours call or a patient emergency, verify the patient's advance directive status by accessing the EHR before providing clinical direction to the patient or caregiver. Never direct a caregiver to call 911 — or direct them not to call 911 — without first verifying the patient's advance directive status.	When responding to any patient emergency.

6.3 Honoring Advance Directives in Emergency Situations

Step	Responsible Party	Action	Timeframe

6.3.1	Clinician Present at Emergency	If a patient's condition deteriorates to the point of cardiac or respiratory arrest in the clinician's presence: (a) immediately assess the patient's advance directive status — specifically, whether there is a POLST specifying No CPR or a DNR order; (b) if a valid POLST or DNR is in effect specifying No CPR: do not initiate CPR; provide comfort measures; call the patient's physician; notify the patient's healthcare agent or caregiver; document all actions; (c) if no advance directive exists or if the advance directive does not address CPR: call 911 immediately and begin CPR while awaiting emergency services if clinically trained and within scope.	Immediately during the emergency.

6.3.2	Clinician Present at Emergency	If a patient has a POLST specifying "Comfort-Focused Treatment" (no hospitalization unless necessary for comfort): do not call 911 for hospital transfer unless the patient's POLST explicitly permits it or the patient's physical suffering cannot be managed at home. Contact the physician and, if the patient is on hospice, the hospice on-call nurse immediately.	During the emergency.

6.3.3	Clinician Present at Emergency	Document the emergency event in the clinical record within 24 hours of the event, including: the patient's condition at the time; the advance directive status; the actions taken; the basis for those actions; and the outcome. File an incident report per RM-ER-002.	Within 24 hours.

6.3.4	Director of Nursing	Review all emergency events involving advance directives within 48 hours to ensure that the patient's directives were honored correctly. If there is any doubt about whether the clinician's response was consistent with the advance directive, consult Legal Counsel and the Compliance Officer.	Within 48 hours of the event.

6.4 Advance Directive Conflicts

Step	Responsible Party	Action	Timeframe

6.4.1	Assigned RN	If a conflict is identified between the patient's advance directive and the patient's current expressed wishes (e.g., a patient with a DNR is now asking for CPR, or a patient who previously created a POLST is now saying they want aggressive treatment), document the conflict and notify the Director of Nursing immediately. A patient with decision-making capacity may verbally revoke any advance directive at any time. Document the revocation and notify the physician within 24 hours.	Immediately upon identification.

6.4.2	Assigned RN	If a conflict is identified between the patient's advance directive and the caregiver's or family member's wishes (e.g., a family member insisting on CPR for a patient with a No CPR POLST), document the conflict and notify the Director of Nursing immediately. The patient's advance directive governs — not the family member's wishes — unless the patient has revoked the directive.	Immediately upon identification.

6.4.3	Director of Nursing	Facilitate a goals-of-care conversation involving the physician, the patient (if able), the healthcare agent (if designated), and the relevant family members to resolve the conflict. Document the conversation and the outcome.	Within 48 hours of identification.

6.4.4	Director of Nursing / Administrator	If the conflict cannot be resolved through clinical discussion and the advance directive's validity or scope is genuinely uncertain, consult Legal Counsel. Do not override a legally valid advance directive without legal guidance.	Consultation within 5 business days of identification.

6.5 Clinician Objections to Advance Directives

Step	Responsible Party	Action	Timeframe

6.5.1	Any Clinician	If a clinician has a personal moral, ethical, or religious objection to honoring a specific advance directive — for example, a clinician who believes life-sustaining treatment should always be provided — the clinician shall disclose the objection to the Director of Nursing and shall not allow the objection to affect their care of the patient or their honoring of the advance directive. The patient's advance directive shall be honored regardless of the clinician's personal beliefs.	Immediately upon identification of the conflict.

6.5.2	Director of Nursing	Reassign the patient to a clinician without the objection, if feasible, to eliminate the potential for the objection to affect care delivery. Document the reassignment and the reason.	Within 48 hours of disclosure.

6.6 Escalation and Exception Handling

Condition	Escalation Path	Corrective Action	Timeframe

A clinician provides CPR to a patient with a valid No CPR POLST	Director of Nursing and Compliance Officer	Director of Nursing and Compliance Officer review the event. Assess whether the clinician was aware of the POLST. Determine whether the agency's advance directive information system failed. Provide corrective training. Report to the Governing Body at the next quarterly meeting.	Review within 24 hours; corrective actions within 14 calendar days.

A patient's advance directive has not been filed in the clinical record despite documentation that the patient has one	Medical Records and Director of Nursing	Director of Nursing directs the assigned RN to obtain a copy of the directive immediately and file it in the EHR. Until the document is filed, place an urgent alert on the patient's record.	Document obtained within 48 hours.

A patient's POLST is not in a visible location in the home	Assigned RN	Clinician educates the patient and caregiver on POLST placement (refrigerator door), assists with placement at the visit, and documents the education and placement confirmation.	During the visit.

7. Documentation Requirements

Requirement	Document / Record	Responsible Party	Location	Timeframe

Advance directive assessment at SOC	Advance directive type, content summary, document location in the home	Assigned RN	EHR — Assessment module; Folder 03	At SOC; retained 7 years

Copy of advance directive document	Filed copy of patient's advance directive	Medical Records	EHR — Folder 03	Within 5 business days of SOC

OASIS advance directive item (M1051)	Accurately coded per CMS guidance	Assigned RN	EHR — OASIS module	At each OASIS time point

EHR advance directive alert	Active alert for all patients with active advance directives	IT Director / Director of Nursing	EHR — patient record header	From SOC throughout episode

Advance directive information provision	Documentation that written information was provided to patients without advance directives	Assigned RN	EHR — clinical record	At SOC

Emergency event documentation	Event note per Section 6.3.3; incident report	Assigned RN	EHR — clinical notes; Incident reporting system	Within 24 hours of event

Advance directive conflict documentation	Conflict identified, notification, and resolution	Assigned RN / Director of Nursing	EHR — coordination notes	At each event; retained 7 years

Policy acknowledgment	Signed acknowledgment — Appendix A	Each individual; Administrator (collection)	Policy acknowledgment file	Within 14 calendar days

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

Compliance Indicator	Measurement Method	Acceptable Standard

Advance directive status documented at SOC	Chart audit	≥98% of SOC assessments with advance directive status documented

OASIS M1051 consistent with advance directive documentation	OASIS-to-narrative cross-reference audit	≥95% consistency

Copy of advance directive filed in clinical record for all patients with advance directives	Medical Records quarterly audit	≥95%

EHR advance directive alert active for all patients with active advance directives	EHR configuration audit	100%

No documented instances of advance directive not being honored	Director of Nursing emergency event review	Zero violations

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors under Tag G163 (42 CFR § 484.50) will verify that: every patient's advance directive status is documented; copies of advance directives are in the clinical record; and that clinical staff can describe the process for honoring a patient's advance directive in an emergency. Surveyors may interview patients to verify they were asked about advance directives and that they received information about their right to create one.

8.3 Common Failure Points

Failure Point	Risk	Mitigation

POLST exists but is not in a visible location in the home	Emergency responders may not find it and may initiate CPR contrary to the patient's wishes	POLST placement education at every SOC; placement confirmation documented in visit note

Patient has a DPAHC but the healthcare agent's contact information is not in the clinical record	Healthcare agent cannot be reached in an emergency	Require healthcare agent name, relationship, and contact information as mandatory clinical record elements

Advance directive status assessed at SOC but not reassessed at RECERT	Patient may have updated or revoked the directive	RECERT assessment template includes advance directive reassessment as a required field

On-call staff unaware of patient's POLST	Wrong emergency response	EHR alert visible on patient's record; on-call staff trained to check EHR advance directive alert before any emergency guidance

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All clinical staff shall receive training on advance directive requirements within 14 calendar days of hire and annually thereafter, including: the types of advance directives recognized in California; the SOC advance directive assessment process; POLST placement and emergency recognition; honoring advance directives in emergency situations; the conflict resolution process; and the prohibition on allowing personal beliefs to override patient directives.

10.2 On-call nursing staff shall receive specific training on advance directive emergency response — specifically, the process for verifying a patient's POLST status before providing guidance during an after-hours emergency.

10.3 All personnel within scope shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix A) within 14 calendar days.

CL-PR-003 — Informed Consent

1. Policy Header

Field	Value

Policy ID	CL-PR-003

Policy Title	Informed Consent

Domain	CL — Clinical Operations

Subdomain	PR — Patient Rights & Safety

Classification Tier	REQUIRED

Status	ACTIVE

Review Cycle	Annual

Access Tier	Tier 2 — Restricted

Policy Owner / Steward	Director of Nursing

Effective Date	2025-07-10

Version	1.0

Approved By	Governing Body Chair — Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc.

Last Reviewed	2025-07-10

Next Review Date	2026-07-10

Supersedes	N/A (Initial Version)

2. Purpose

This policy establishes the requirements for obtaining and documenting informed consent for all home health services at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. Informed consent is the embodiment of patient autonomy — the principle that a person has the right to make decisions about what happens to their own body, and that those decisions can only be truly voluntary when they are made with adequate information. Informed consent in home health is not a one-time signature on an admission form. It is an ongoing process of communication, understanding, and patient decision-making that extends throughout the entire home health episode.

The legal doctrine of informed consent requires that before a clinician performs any procedure or treatment, the patient must be provided with: a description of the proposed service or procedure; the clinical reason the service is recommended; the expected benefits; the material risks; reasonable alternatives; and the consequences of refusing. The patient must be given an opportunity to ask questions and must provide voluntary consent — consent that is not the product of pressure, deception, or undue influence. And the consent must be documented.

In the home health context, the informed consent process carries dimensions not present in institutional care: the patient is in their home, which may create a power dynamic favoring compliance; the clinical team visits intermittently rather than continuously, which means consent discussions must be documented precisely because they cannot be witnessed by institutional staff; and patients with cognitive impairment or language barriers require additional care to ensure that consent is genuinely informed and genuinely voluntary. This policy establishes the informed consent framework that governs every clinical service at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc., and the documentation standards that make that consent legally defensible and clinically meaningful.

3. Scope

This policy applies to:

All clinical disciplines (RN, LVN, PT, OT, SLP, MSW, HHA) seeking and documenting patient consent before clinical services

Intake staff who obtain general consent for home health services at admission

The Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager

All contracted clinical staff providing services under agency authorization

This policy governs consent for home health services generally (general consent at admission) and consent for specific clinical procedures (procedure-specific informed consent). It does not govern consent in research contexts. It does not govern consent for services beyond the agency's scope.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. shall obtain and document informed consent from every patient — or the patient's legally authorized representative — before initiating home health services and before performing any specific clinical procedure or intervention for which the patient's voluntary and informed decision is required.

4.2 Informed consent is valid only when: (a) the patient has been provided with sufficient information to make an informed decision, including the nature of the proposed service, its purpose, its expected benefits, its material risks, available alternatives, and the consequence of refusal; (b) the patient has decision-making capacity; (c) the consent is voluntary — not the product of pressure, coercion, or undue influence; and (d) the patient communicates agreement to receive the proposed service.

4.3 The informed consent process shall occur in the patient's primary language. If an interpreter is required, interpreter services shall be arranged per OP-PA-003 before the consent discussion begins. Consent obtained from a patient who did not understand the consent discussion is not valid informed consent.

4.4 A patient who lacks decision-making capacity shall have consent obtained from their legally authorized representative — the healthcare agent designated in a DPAHC, or the surrogate decision-maker per California's surrogate priority list under Probate Code § 4711 et seq. The representative's authority to consent shall be verified and documented.

4.5 The agency's general consent for home health services shall be obtained at admission using the General Consent for Home Health Services form (Appendix A). The general consent covers routine home health services as described in the plan of care. It does not constitute informed consent for specific high-risk procedures, which require a separate procedure-specific consent discussion.

4.6 Procedure-specific informed consent discussions are required before performing the following services for the first time with any patient: IV therapy and infusion services; central line care procedures; wound care involving debridement or advanced wound care techniques; any procedure not previously received by the patient in the home setting that carries material risk; and any procedure that the patient has previously questioned or expressed uncertainty about.

4.7 A patient may withdraw consent at any time, for any reason, without adverse consequence to the quality of care provided. When consent is withdrawn, the clinician shall document the withdrawal, the patient's stated reason, the education provided regarding clinical implications, and the plan to address the patient's clinical needs without the refused service.

4.8 The agency shall maintain a record of all consents and withdrawals of consent in the patient's clinical record. The consent record shall include the date, the service or procedure consented to, the patient's or representative's signature, and documentation that the required consent elements were communicated.

5. Definitions

Term	Definition

Informed Consent	The legally and ethically valid process by which a patient with decision-making capacity voluntarily agrees to a proposed medical service or procedure after having received, understood, and considered sufficient information about the service — including its nature, purpose, benefits, risks, alternatives, and the consequence of refusal.

Decision-Making Capacity	The ability of a patient to understand the information provided about a proposed service or procedure, appreciate the consequences of accepting or refusing, reason about the options, and communicate a choice. Distinguished from legal competency, which is a court determination.

General Consent	A broadly framed consent obtained at admission that authorizes routine home health services as described in the plan of care. It does not substitute for procedure-specific informed consent for high-risk or novel procedures.

Procedure-Specific Informed Consent	A consent obtained immediately before a specific, higher-risk, or novel clinical procedure that includes all required consent elements specific to that procedure.

Material Risk	A risk that a reasonable patient in the patient's circumstances would consider significant in making a decision about whether to consent to a proposed service. Materiality is determined from the patient's perspective, not the clinician's.

Voluntary	Consent that is given freely, without pressure, coercion, deception, manipulation, or undue influence.

Assent	The expression of willingness to receive care by a patient who lacks full decision-making capacity (including children and some cognitively impaired adults) — not a substitute for consent from the authorized decision-maker, but an important ethical and clinical consideration.

Surrogate Decision-Maker	A person authorized under California Probate Code to make healthcare decisions for a patient who lacks capacity when no healthcare agent has been designated. The surrogate priority list is: spouse or domestic partner; adult child; parent; adult sibling; other adult relative; close adult friend.

Withdrawal of Consent	The patient's exercise of the right to revoke a previously given consent, at any time, for any reason, without adverse consequence to care quality.

6. Procedures

6.1 General Consent for Home Health Services — Admission

Step	Responsible Party	Action	Timeframe

6.1.1	Intake Staff / Assigned RN	At or prior to the first visit, present the General Consent for Home Health Services form (Appendix A) to the patient and review its content verbally. The review shall cover: the scope of services described in the patient's plan of care; the agency's service delivery approach; the patient's right to refuse any service or visit at any time; the agency's billing and insurance verification; and the patient's right to access their clinical record.	At or before the first visit.

6.1.2	Intake Staff / Assigned RN	Ensure the consent discussion occurs in the patient's primary language. If an interpreter is needed, arrange interpretation per OP-PA-003 before beginning the discussion. Document the language used and, if interpretation was provided, the interpreter's name.	Before the consent discussion.

6.1.3	Intake Staff / Assigned RN	Provide the patient and/or legally authorized representative an opportunity to ask questions about the services described in the general consent and about the consent form itself. Answer all questions honestly and completely.	During the consent discussion.

6.1.4	Intake Staff / Assigned RN	Obtain the patient's or legally authorized representative's signature on the General Consent form. If the patient is unable to sign due to a physical limitation, document the reason and the alternative acknowledgment method. If the patient lacks decision-making capacity, obtain consent from the legally authorized representative — verify the representative's authority and document the verification.	At the consent discussion; before the first visit.

6.1.5	Medical Records	File the signed General Consent form in the patient's clinical record (EHR — Folder 01, Admission & Intake). Flag the clinical record if a signed consent form is not present within 5 business days of the SOC.	Within 5 business days of SOC.

6.2 Procedure-Specific Informed Consent

Step	Responsible Party	Action	Timeframe

6.2.1	Assigned Clinician	Before performing any specific procedure requiring procedure-specific consent (per Section 4.6), conduct a procedure-specific consent discussion with the patient. The discussion shall cover: (a) the name and nature of the procedure; (b) the clinical reason the procedure is recommended; (c) the expected benefit of the procedure; (d) the material risks of the procedure — those risks that the patient would find significant in deciding whether to consent; (e) reasonable alternatives to the procedure, including the option of not performing it; (f) the consequence of declining the procedure — what would happen clinically if the patient chose not to have it.	Before each procedure requiring specific consent; documented in the visit note.

6.2.2	Assigned Clinician	Conduct the procedure-specific consent discussion in plain language. Avoid medical jargon that the patient may not understand. After explaining each element, check the patient's understanding by asking them to restate in their own words what they understood — this is a teach-back component of the consent process.	During the consent discussion.

6.2.3	Assigned Clinician	Allow the patient adequate time to consider the information before asking for a consent decision. Do not pressure the patient for an immediate decision unless there is an urgent clinical need that precludes waiting. Document the time provided for consideration in the visit note.	During the consent discussion.

6.2.4	Assigned Clinician	Document the procedure-specific consent discussion in the visit note for that visit, including: (a) the procedure discussed; (b) the specific elements of the consent discussion (nature, purpose, benefits, risks, alternatives, consequence of refusal); (c) the patient's demonstrated understanding via teach-back; (d) the patient's decision (consent given, consent declined); (e) if consent was declined, the education provided about clinical implications. A signature for procedure-specific consent is not separately required — the clinician's documentation of the discussion in the authenticated visit note constitutes the consent record for home health clinical procedures. For high-acuity procedures such as IV therapy initiation, a Procedure-Specific Consent Notation (Appendix B) is recommended as an adjunct to the visit note documentation.	In the visit note for the relevant visit; within 24 hours.

6.2.5	Assigned Clinician	Repeat the procedure-specific consent discussion for subsequent uses of the same procedure if: (a) the patient has previously expressed uncertainty or questions about the procedure; (b) the procedure has been significantly modified; (c) a new material risk has been identified; or (d) the patient's clinical condition has changed in a way that affects the risk-benefit analysis.	As indicated by the above criteria.

6.3 Assessing and Documenting Decision-Making Capacity

Step	Responsible Party	Action	Timeframe

6.3.1	Assigned RN	When there is clinical reason to question whether a patient has decision-making capacity — because of dementia, delirium, severe psychiatric illness, intoxication, or other condition — assess the patient's capacity before obtaining consent. The assessment shall evaluate: (a) can the patient understand the information provided? (b) can the patient appreciate how the information applies to their situation? (c) can the patient reason about the choices? (d) can the patient communicate a choice? A patient who can do all four has decision-making capacity for the specific decision at hand.	Whenever capacity is in question.

6.3.2	Assigned RN	Document the capacity assessment in the clinical record, including the clinical findings that inform the assessment. If the patient is found to lack capacity: (a) identify the legally authorized representative; (b) contact the representative to obtain consent; (c) document the representative's name, relationship, and the consent provided.	At the time of the capacity assessment.

6.3.3	Assigned RN	Note that decision-making capacity is not all-or-nothing — a patient may have capacity for simple decisions but not complex ones, or may have capacity on some days and not others (fluctuating capacity due to delirium). Document the scope of the patient's capacity and make consent decisions accordingly.	On an ongoing basis for patients with fluctuating capacity.

6.3.4	Director of Nursing	When a capacity question involves a clinical procedure with significant consequences — such as refusal of IV antibiotics for a serious infection — consult with the attending physician and, if appropriate, Legal Counsel. Document all consultations.	Within 24 hours of identification.

6.4 Consent Withdrawal

Step	Responsible Party	Action	Timeframe

6.4.1	All Clinicians	When a patient withdraws consent for any service, visit, or procedure, honor the withdrawal immediately. Do not pressure the patient, do not argue, and do not threaten any adverse consequences for the withdrawal.	Immediately upon withdrawal.

6.4.2	All Clinicians	Document the withdrawal of consent in the clinical record: the service or procedure for which consent was withdrawn; the patient's stated reason (if provided); the education delivered about clinical implications; the plan to address the patient's needs without the refused service; and the physician notification status.	In the visit note for the relevant visit; within 24 hours.

6.4.3	Assigned RN	Notify the physician within 24 hours of any consent withdrawal that affects the patient's medical plan of care or creates a clinical safety concern. Obtain physician guidance on how to manage the patient's clinical needs given the withdrawal.	Within 24 hours of withdrawal.

6.5 Consent in Pediatric Patients

Step	Responsible Party	Action	Timeframe

6.5.1	Intake Staff / Assigned RN	For all pediatric patients (under age 18), obtain consent from the patient's parent or legal guardian — not from the minor patient. Verify the consenting individual's legal relationship to the patient and document the verification.	At admission; before each consent-required procedure.

6.5.2	Assigned Clinician	For pediatric patients with sufficient developmental capacity to understand and participate — generally school-age children and adolescents — seek the patient's assent in addition to the parent's or guardian's consent. Explain the procedure to the child in age-appropriate language and ask if they are willing to receive the service. Document the assent.	Before procedures for applicable patients.

6.5.3	Assigned RN	For emancipated minors — patients under 18 who are legally emancipated under California Family Code § 7002 — obtain consent from the minor patient directly. Verify emancipated status documentation and file a copy in the clinical record.	At admission for emancipated minors.

6.6 Escalation and Exception Handling

Condition	Escalation Path	Corrective Action	Timeframe

Patient declines to sign the general consent but verbally agrees to receive services	Assigned RN documents verbal consent	Document the verbal consent in detail — the discussion, the patient's verbal agreement, and the reason a signature was not obtained (refusal to sign, physical inability). The agency may proceed with services based on documented verbal consent.	In the visit note.

Procedure is performed without documentation of informed consent discussion	Director of Nursing identifies during audit	Director of Nursing reviews the clinical record to assess whether consent discussion was had but not documented. If the discussion occurred, direct the clinician to create a late entry amendment per CO-DC-003. If the discussion did not occur, assess whether the lack of consent constitutes a patient rights violation.	Assessment within 5 business days; corrective action within 14 calendar days.

A legally authorized representative refuses a service that the patient (who has capacity) wants	Director of Nursing	If the patient has decision-making capacity, the patient's consent governs — not the representative's. Educate the representative about the patient's right to consent. Document all communications. If the conflict persists, consult Legal Counsel.	Resolution within 48 hours.

7. Documentation Requirements

Requirement	Document / Record	Responsible Party	Location	Timeframe

General Consent for Home Health Services	Signed consent form per Appendix A	Intake Staff / Assigned RN (discussion); patient/representative (signature)	EHR — Folder 01	At or before the first visit; retained 7 years

Procedure-specific consent discussion	Documentation in visit note covering all required consent elements per Section 6.2.4	Assigned Clinician	EHR — visit note	In the visit note for the relevant visit; within 24 hours

Procedure-Specific Consent Notation (for high-acuity procedures)	Notation form per Appendix B	Assigned Clinician	EHR — visit note adjunct	At the time of the procedure; retained 7 years

Capacity assessment	Clinical record entry documenting capacity assessment findings	Assigned RN	EHR — clinical notes	At the time of assessment

Consent withdrawal documentation	Documentation in visit note per Section 6.4.2	All Clinicians	EHR — visit note	In the visit note for the relevant visit; within 24 hours

Legally authorized representative verification	Copy of guardianship order, DPAHC, or equivalent	Assigned RN / Medical Records	EHR — Folder 01	When representative designation is identified

Policy acknowledgment	Signed acknowledgment — Appendix C	Each individual; Administrator (collection)	Policy acknowledgment file	Within 14 calendar days

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

Compliance Indicator	Measurement Method	Acceptable Standard

Signed General Consent on file for every patient	Medical Records quarterly completeness audit	100%

Procedure-specific consent discussion documented in visit note for first IV therapy visit	Chart audit of all IV therapy SOC visits	≥95%

Consent withdrawal documented with required elements	Chart audit of records with documented refusals	≥95%

Capacity assessment documented for all patients with identified capacity concerns	Chart audit	≥95%

Consent obtained from legally authorized representative for all incapacitated patients	Chart audit	100%

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors under Tag G165 (42 CFR § 484.50 — informed consent component of patient rights) will verify that: consent is obtained before services begin; consent is documented in the clinical record; patients are informed of their right to refuse; and refusals are honored and documented. Surveyors may interview patients to determine whether they understood what they consented to and whether they felt free to refuse.

8.3 Common Failure Points

Failure Point	Risk	Mitigation

General consent obtained but no record in clinical record	Missing required documentation; billing vulnerability	Medical Records verification within 5 business days of SOC

IV therapy initiated without documented procedure-specific consent discussion	Patient rights deficiency; liability exposure	EHR workflow requiring consent documentation before IV therapy visit note can be locked

Consent obtained from a family member who is not the legally authorized representative	Legally invalid consent	Train intake and clinical staff on representative verification; require documentation of legal authority

Procedure-specific consent discussion not conducted in the patient's primary language	Invalid consent if patient did not understand	Interpreter services required for all consent discussions; documented in the clinical record

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All clinical staff shall receive training on informed consent requirements within 14 calendar days of hire and annually thereafter, including: the elements of valid informed consent; the procedure-specific consent discussion process; the teach-back approach to verifying patient understanding; assessing decision-making capacity; obtaining consent from legally authorized representatives; pediatric consent and assent; and documenting consent in the clinical record.

10.2 Intake staff shall receive training on the general consent process — provision of the general consent form, reviewing its content, and obtaining signature — within 14 calendar days of hire.

10.3 All personnel within scope shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix C) within 14 calendar days.

CL-PR-004 — Restraint & Seclusion Prohibition

1. Policy Header

Field	Value

Policy ID	CL-PR-004

Policy Title	Restraint & Seclusion Prohibition

Domain	CL — Clinical Operations

Subdomain	PR — Patient Rights & Safety

Classification Tier	REQUIRED

Status	ACTIVE

Review Cycle	Annual

Access Tier	Tier 2 — Restricted

Policy Owner / Steward	Director of Nursing

Effective Date	2025-07-10

Version	1.0

Approved By	Governing Body Chair — Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc.

Last Reviewed	2025-07-10

Next Review Date	2026-07-10

Supersedes	N/A (Initial Version)

2. Purpose

This policy prohibits the use of restraints or seclusion in the home health setting and defines protocols for managing unsafe patient situations at Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. The prohibition on restraints and seclusion in home health is absolute — it is not a clinical preference, a guideline, or an approach to be balanced against other considerations. It is a Condition of Participation under 42 CFR § 484.50(c)(9), which specifically states that a patient has the right to be free from any physical or chemical restraints imposed for purposes of discipline or convenience and not required to treat the patient's medical symptoms.

In a hospital or skilled nursing facility, restraints are sometimes used — within strict regulatory and clinical protocols — in emergency situations where a patient poses an immediate danger to themselves or others. In the home health setting, there is no analogous justification. Agency staff do not have the authority to restrain a patient in their own home. If a patient is behaving in a manner that creates an immediate safety threat to agency staff or themselves, the agency clinician's options are not to restrain — they are to leave the home and call for emergency assistance.

This does not mean that home health clinicians are helpless in the face of patient safety challenges. The clinical management of patients with dementia-related behavioral symptoms, fall risk, wandering risk, agitation, or other conditions that might tempt institutional providers toward restraint use has robust, evidence-based alternatives in the home health context — environmental modifications, behavioral redirection, medication management, caregiver training, alarm systems, and removal of hazards. These alternatives are clinically superior and legally required. This policy establishes both the absolute prohibition and the clinical alternatives that give it operational meaning.

3. Scope

This policy applies to:

All agency personnel who provide direct care to patients — including all clinical disciplines and home health aides

All contracted clinical staff providing services under agency authorization

The Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager

The Compliance Officer for oversight of restraint prohibition compliance

Caregivers and families who may seek to use restraints at the direction of, or with the knowledge of, agency staff

4. Policy Statement

4.1 Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. prohibits the use of physical restraints — any manual method, physical hold, or mechanical device that restricts freedom of movement — for any patient receiving home health services, for any purpose, under any circumstance. This prohibition is absolute and applies to all agency personnel.

4.2 Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. prohibits the use of chemical restraints — any medication used as a restraint to manage behavior and not required to treat the patient's medical symptoms — for any patient receiving home health services. The use of a medication authorized by the patient's physician for the clinical management of a symptom (e.g., antipsychotics for dementia-related psychosis; benzodiazepines for anxiety; opioids for pain) is not a chemical restraint. A medication is a chemical restraint when it is used primarily to control behavior for the convenience of caregivers or clinical staff rather than to treat a clinical condition.

4.3 Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. prohibits involuntary seclusion — confining a patient to a room or area against their will, or isolating a patient from other persons — for any purpose.

4.4 No agency staff member shall recommend, suggest, direct, or acquiesce to the use of restraints or seclusion by a patient's caregiver or family, regardless of the clinical circumstances. If a caregiver asks about restraints, the clinician shall educate the caregiver about the prohibition and about clinical alternatives.

4.5 When a patient's condition or behavior creates a risk of harm to the patient or to agency staff, the clinician's response shall follow the safety protocols defined in Section 6.3 of this policy — not restraint. Physical restraint of a patient by an agency clinician in the patient's home is never an appropriate clinical response.

4.6 When agency staff discover that a caregiver is using physical restraints on a patient (e.g., tying the patient to a bed or chair, locking the patient in a room), the clinician shall treat this as potential abuse or neglect, document the finding, notify the Director of Nursing immediately, and initiate the mandatory reporting process per CL-PR-006 and HR-ER-009.

4.7 Certain devices that limit movement are not classified as restraints and may be appropriate in the home health setting when ordered by a physician for a clinical purpose: bed rails (when ordered and documented as a therapeutic medical device, not for confinement); mitts used to prevent self-extubation in the immediate post-operative period (when ordered and monitored); splints and orthotic devices; positioning devices. These are governed by the physician's clinical orders and are not affected by this policy.

5. Definitions

Term	Definition

Physical Restraint	Any manual method, physical or mechanical device, material, or equipment that immobilizes or reduces the ability of a patient to move their arms, legs, body, or head freely. Includes: vest restraints; wrist restraints; mitts (when used for behavioral management); belt restraints; full side rails that prevent the patient from leaving the bed when the patient is capable of and wishes to leave; tying; holding.

Chemical Restraint	A medication used as a restraint to control behavior or restrict freedom of movement when the medication is not required to treat the patient's medical symptoms. Distinguished from therapeutic medications prescribed to treat a clinical condition.

Involuntary Seclusion	The separation of a patient from others, or the confinement of a patient to a room or space, against the patient's will or without the patient's consent, for any purpose.

Therapeutic Medical Device	A device ordered by a physician for a specific clinical purpose that incidentally limits movement but is not used as a restraint — including surgical dressings, IV lines, urinary catheters, braces, splints, supportive positioning devices, and medically necessary bed rails.

Behavioral Intervention	A non-pharmacologic, non-restraint technique for managing a patient's distressing or unsafe behavior — including environmental modification, redirection, validation therapy, structured activity, caregiver training, and de-escalation communication.

De-Escalation	A set of verbal and non-verbal communication techniques designed to reduce a patient's agitation, fear, or distress and to prevent escalation of unsafe behavior — without the use of physical force or restraint.

6. Procedures

6.1 Preventing Restraint Use — Assessment and Alternatives

Step	Responsible Party	Action	Timeframe

6.1.1	Assigned RN	At the SOC visit, assess the patient for conditions that historically lead caregivers or clinical staff to consider restraints — including dementia with behavioral symptoms (agitation, wandering, combativeness), fall risk, delirium, post-surgical disorientation, and severe cognitive impairment. Document the assessment.	At the SOC visit.

6.1.2	Assigned RN	For every patient identified with conditions that carry restraint risk: (a) develop a non-restraint behavioral and safety management plan as part of the plan of care; (b) educate the patient and caregiver on the restraint prohibition and on the clinical alternatives; (c) refer to the MSW for psychosocial assessment and community resource linkage if caregiver stress or safety management is a concern; (d) refer to PT/OT for fall prevention and environmental modification assessment per CL-SD-015 and RM-PS-001 if mobility safety is a concern.	Within 24 hours of SOC assessment.

6.1.3	Assigned RN	When a caregiver asks about using restraints for a patient — for example, "Can we tie him to the chair so he doesn't fall?" — respond clearly: (a) explain that restraints are prohibited and that the clinician cannot recommend or authorize their use; (b) explain the clinical and safety risks of restraints — including pressure injuries, deconditioning, falls when attempting to escape, increased agitation, and aspiration; (c) provide education on evidence-based alternatives for the specific concern the caregiver has raised; (d) document the caregiver education in the clinical record.	When the question is raised.

6.1.4	Assigned RN	Non-restraint alternatives for specific conditions (to be individualized to the patient's specific situation and ordered by the physician where appropriate): (a) Fall risk — refer to PT for balance and gait training; recommend grab bars, non-slip surfaces, adequate lighting, removal of throw rugs; assess for and recommend appropriate assistive device; medication review for fall-risk medications per CL-SD-015; (b) Wandering / elopement risk — refer to RM-PS-004; recommend door alarms, bed alarms, GPS monitoring devices per physician order; structured activity to reduce wandering triggers; (c) Agitation / combativeness — behavioral redirection; structured routine; pain assessment per CL-SD-014; behavioral health assessment per CL-SD-022; physician consultation for medication management of behavioral symptoms; (d) Self-harm risk — behavioral health assessment; safety planning per CL-SD-022 Section 6.5; physician consultation; increased visit frequency.	As clinically indicated; individualized to the patient.

6.2 Staff Response When a Patient Refuses Care or Becomes Unsafe During a Visit

Step	Responsible Party	Action	Timeframe

6.2.1	Visiting Clinician	When a patient refuses care during a visit — including pulling away from a procedure, verbally refusing to cooperate, or physically moving away from the clinician — stop the procedure immediately. Physical restraint to continue a procedure against the patient's active refusal is assault. Respect the refusal.	Immediately upon refusal.

6.2.2	Visiting Clinician	When a patient becomes confused or disoriented during a visit and is attempting to leave the home unsafely or to engage in behavior that could harm them: (a) attempt verbal de-escalation — speak calmly, in simple language, without approaching rapidly or touching without permission; (b) engage the caregiver to assist with redirection if the caregiver is present; (c) assess the patient for an acute medical cause of the behavioral change (hypoglycemia, delirium, medication adverse effect, pain, urinary retention); (d) if the patient cannot be safely redirected and is in immediate danger, call 911 for assistance. Do not physically restrain the patient.	During the clinical situation.

6.2.3	Visiting Clinician	When a patient or caregiver poses a physical threat to the clinician's safety: leave the home immediately. Do not remain in an unsafe environment and do not attempt to physically control the situation. Call 911 if there is an immediate threat of violence. Notify the Director of Nursing within 2 hours. Document the event.	Immediately upon identification of threat.

6.2.4	Visiting Clinician	After any situation involving a patient's unsafe behavior, document: the patient's behavior; the clinical assessment of its potential cause; the de-escalation techniques applied; the patient's response; any emergency services contacted; the outcome. Notify the Director of Nursing within 2 hours of any significant behavioral event.	Within 24 hours; Director of Nursing notification within 2 hours.

6.3 Discovery of Restraint Use by Caregiver

Step	Responsible Party	Action	Timeframe

6.3.1	Visiting Clinician	If a clinician discovers that a caregiver is using physical restraints on a patient — including tying the patient to a bed, chair, or other object; using bed rails that prevent movement and are not medically ordered; or confining the patient to a room — document the finding in the visit note immediately.	During the visit.

6.3.2	Visiting Clinician	Assess the patient for any physical harm resulting from the restraint: skin integrity at the restraint site; circulation; respiratory status; presence of pressure injury; patient's subjective report of distress. Document the assessment.	During the visit.

6.3.3	Visiting Clinician	Educate the caregiver about the prohibition on restraints, the clinical risks of restraints, and the evidence-based alternatives. Request that the caregiver discontinue the restraint and document the education and the caregiver's response.	During the visit.

6.3.4	Visiting Clinician	Notify the Director of Nursing within 2 hours of the discovery. The Director of Nursing shall assess whether the restraint use constitutes abuse or neglect requiring mandatory reporting per CL-PR-006 and HR-ER-009.	Within 2 hours.

6.3.5	Director of Nursing	Consult with the Compliance Officer and, if indicated, initiate a mandatory abuse/neglect report to the appropriate California authority per CL-PR-006. Document all actions.	Within 4 hours of Director of Nursing notification.

6.3.6	Assigned RN / Director of Nursing	Update the plan of care to include: explicit instruction to the caregiver regarding the prohibition on restraints; the specific behavioral and safety management plan that provides alternatives; and increased visit frequency if clinically warranted to monitor the patient's safety.	Within 24 hours of discovery.

6.4 Escalation and Exception Handling

Condition	Escalation Path	Corrective Action	Timeframe

An agency clinician is found to have used a physical restraint on a patient	Director of Nursing and Compliance Officer	Director of Nursing immediately removes the clinician from patient care. Compliance Officer initiates a compliance investigation per CO-CP-007. Assess for mandatory reporting requirements. If the use of restraints constitutes abuse or neglect, report to CDPH per CL-PR-006.	Clinician removed from patient care immediately; investigation within 24 hours.

A physician orders a physical restraint for a home health patient	Director of Nursing and Compliance Officer	The agency shall not execute a physician order for physical restraints in the home health setting — this is outside the agency's scope and violates the Conditions of Participation. Director of Nursing contacts the physician to explain the prohibition and to discuss alternative clinical management strategies. Document all communications.	Physician contact within 24 hours.

A caregiver continues to use restraints after being educated about the prohibition	Director of Nursing and Compliance Officer	Initiate mandatory abuse/neglect report if caregiver restraint use constitutes abuse per CL-PR-006. Director of Nursing assesses whether it is safe to continue providing services in the home environment. Consider safety-related discharge per CL-CP-006 Section 6.4 if the patient's safety cannot be assured.	Mandatory report immediately if indicated; safety assessment within 48 hours.

7. Documentation Requirements

Requirement	Document / Record	Responsible Party	Location	Timeframe

Restraint risk assessment at SOC	Assessment findings and non-restraint management plan	Assigned RN	EHR — assessment module	At SOC; retained 7 years

Caregiver education on restraint prohibition	Education content, method, and caregiver response	Assigned RN	EHR — visit note	When education is provided; within 24 hours

Discovery of caregiver restraint use	Clinical assessment of patient; education provided; Director of Nursing notification	Visiting Clinician	EHR — visit note; incident report per RM-ER-002	During or immediately following the visit; Director of Nursing within 2 hours

Unsafe patient behavior incident	Behavior description; de-escalation techniques; outcome; emergency services (if called)	Visiting Clinician	EHR — visit note; incident report per RM-ER-002	Within 24 hours; Director of Nursing within 2 hours

Mandatory abuse/neglect report (if indicated)	SCAR form or equivalent per CL-PR-006	Visiting Clinician; Compliance Officer	Compliance file; CDPH report	Per mandatory reporting timelines

Policy acknowledgment	Signed acknowledgment — Appendix A	Each individual; Administrator (collection)	Policy acknowledgment file	Within 14 calendar days

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

Compliance Indicator	Measurement Method	Acceptable Standard

No documented instances of agency-initiated physical restraint	Director of Nursing review of incident reports and clinical records	Zero violations

Restraint risk assessment completed at SOC for high-risk patients	Chart audit	≥95% of high-risk patients with documented assessment

Caregiver restraint discovery triggers Director of Nursing notification within 2 hours	Director of Nursing log and visit note timestamp audit	100%

Non-restraint behavioral management plan documented for all patients with restraint risk conditions	Chart audit	≥95%

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors under Tag G171 (42 CFR § 484.50(c)(9)) will: review clinical records for evidence of restraint orders or restraint documentation; interview patients about whether they have ever been restrained; interview clinical staff about the restraint prohibition and the alternatives used for patients with behavioral challenges. Surveyors may also review incident reports for any events that could indicate restraint use.

8.3 Common Failure Points

Failure Point	Risk	Mitigation

Clinician discovers caregiver restraint use but does not report or document it	Failure to report potential abuse/neglect; survey citation	Annual training on restraint discovery reporting; mandatory reporting obligation applies to caregiver restraint use

Clinician suggests to caregiver that "something to keep her in the chair" might help with falls	Implicit endorsement of restraint use; patient rights violation	Annual training with specific scenario examples; Director of Nursing zero-tolerance messaging

No non-restraint behavioral alternatives documented for cognitively impaired patients	Clinical gap; increased caregiver likelihood of attempting restraints	SOC assessment required to identify restraint-risk patients and develop management plan

9. References

Citation / Document	Relevance

42 CFR § 484.50(c)(9)	Patient right to be free from restraint and seclusion

CMS State Operations Manual, Appendix B, Tag G171	Survey guidance for restraint and seclusion prohibition

CL-PR-001	Patient Rights & Responsibilities

CL-PR-006	Abuse, Neglect & Exploitation Reporting

CL-SD-015	Fall Risk Assessment & Prevention — non-restraint fall prevention

CL-SD-022	Behavioral Health Screening & Referral

CL-SD-023	Palliative & End-of-Life Care — behavioral management at end of life

HR-ER-009	Mandatory Abuse Reporting by Staff

RM-PS-004	Patient Elopement & Wandering Risk

RM-ER-002	Incident Reporting & Investigation

EN-TG-001	Enterprise Policy Taxonomy & Classification Governance

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All agency personnel who have direct patient contact shall receive training on the restraint prohibition within 14 calendar days of hire and annually thereafter, including: the absolute prohibition on physical and chemical restraints; the definition of a chemical restraint distinguished from a therapeutic medication; the protocol for managing unsafe patient behavior without restraint; de-escalation techniques; the response when caregiver restraint use is discovered; and the mandatory reporting obligation.

10.2 Scenario-based training using realistic home health clinical situations shall be incorporated into annual restraint training to ensure that clinicians can apply the policy in practice — not just recall its provisions.

10.3 All personnel within scope shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix A) within 14 calendar days of the effective date, any revision, or hire/appointment.

11. Version Control

11.1 Maintained per EN-LC-001. Only the most current approved version is valid. 11.2 Substantive revisions require Governing Body approval, re-acknowledgment within 14 calendar days, and policy index update per EN-TG-001. 11.3 Non-substantive revisions approved by Administrator with Governing Body notification.

CL-PR-005 — Emergency Preparedness — Clinical

1. Policy Header

2. Purpose

This policy establishes the clinical protocols, patient-facing emergency communication standards, clinical continuity requirements, and individual patient emergency planning obligations that Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. maintains to protect every patient during emergencies and disasters. Emergency preparedness in home health is not simply a plan that lives in a binder — it is an operational clinical responsibility that activates the moment a disaster is declared, a patient is stranded without caregiver support, a power outage threatens a patient who is technology-dependent, or an infectious disease outbreak disrupts the agency's ability to deliver scheduled visits.

The home health patient population is among the most vulnerable in any emergency: homebound, often without immediate social support, frequently dependent on electricity-powered medical equipment, managing complex medication regimens that require refrigeration or precise timing, and unable to self-evacuate. When a hurricane approaches, when wildfires force evacuations, when a prolonged power outage occurs, or when a pandemic disrupts the workforce — the agency's clinical team is often the only professional lifeline between the patient and a preventable clinical catastrophe.

Federal law requires that Medicare-certified home health agencies maintain an emergency preparedness program under 42 CFR § 484.102. This Condition of Participation requires: a risk assessment-based emergency plan; policies and procedures implementing that plan; a communication plan; and a training and testing program. This policy governs the clinical dimensions of that requirement — specifically, the protocols governing how the agency identifies which patients are most vulnerable in emergencies, communicates with patients during and after a declared emergency, ensures continuity of clinical care for at-risk patients, manages technology-dependent patients, coordinates with community emergency management systems, and documents all emergency-period clinical activities. The agency's operational Emergency Operations and Business Continuity Plan is established in policy OP-FM-005. This policy is the clinical implementation arm of that broader plan.

3. Scope

This policy applies to:

All registered nurses and licensed vocational nurses responsible for assigned patient caseloads

All clinical disciplines (PT, OT, SLP, MSW, HHA) whose patients may be affected by emergencies

The Director of Nursing / Clinical Manager who directs clinical emergency response

The Administrator who activates the Emergency Operations Plan

Clinical Coordinators who manage patient communication and visit scheduling during emergencies

On-call nursing staff who may be the first clinical point of contact during an emergency event

All contracted clinical staff providing services under agency authorization

This policy applies during all declared emergencies — whether declared by federal, state, or local government — and during any event that significantly disrupts the agency's ability to deliver scheduled clinical services, regardless of whether a formal declaration has been issued. It applies to every patient on the active census at the time of the emergency.

4. Policy Statement

4.1 Care Indeed Home Health Care, Inc. shall maintain a continuously updated Patient Emergency Risk Stratification System that classifies every active patient by their level of vulnerability during an emergency, enabling the agency to triage its limited clinical resources to the patients with the greatest clinical need when emergency conditions limit the agency's capacity to serve all patients simultaneously.

4.2 The agency shall maintain current emergency contact information — including at least two emergency contacts beyond the primary caregiver — for every active patient in the clinical record, updated at each visit and verified at each OASIS time point.

4.3 The agency shall develop and maintain an individualized Emergency Preparedness Care Plan (EPCP) for every patient identified as High Risk (Category 1) under the Patient Emergency Risk Stratification System. The EPCP shall be incorporated into the patient's plan of care and shall identify: the specific emergency risks to the patient; the patient's emergency shelter option; the patient's backup caregiver arrangements; the management plan for technology dependence; medication contingency planning; and the patient's designated emergency representative contact.

4.4 During any declared emergency or significant service disruption, the Director of Nursing shall activate the Clinical Emergency Response Protocol, which establishes priority contact sequences, alternative visit delivery mechanisms, and clinical triage decision-making frameworks for managing patient care during the emergency period.

4.5 The agency shall ensure that every technology-dependent patient — including patients using electrically powered medical equipment such as home ventilators, oxygen concentrators, infusion pumps, home dialysis equipment, or feeding pumps — has an individualized technology failure contingency plan documented in the plan of care and in the patient's EPCP.

4.6 The agency shall communicate with all active patients within 24 hours of the onset of any emergency event that disrupts normal service delivery, assess patient safety and clinical status, and document the outcome of every contact attempt.

4.7 The Director of Nursing shall prioritize clinical resources during emergencies based on the Patient Emergency Risk Stratification System, ensuring that Category 1 (Highest Risk) patients receive clinical contact within 12 hours and Category 2 (High Risk) patients receive contact within 24 hours of the emergency onset.

4.8 During an emergency period, the agency shall maintain detailed documentation of: all patient contact attempts and outcomes; clinical decisions made under emergency conditions; any modifications to ordered visit frequency necessitated by the emergency; any patient adverse events that occur during the emergency period; and all coordination with community emergency management agencies, utility companies, and healthcare facilities.

4.9 The agency shall cooperate and coordinate with the local and state emergency management agency, county public health department, and Healthcare Coalition during any declared public health emergency, and shall fulfill reporting obligations under applicable state emergency protocols.

4.10 The agency shall conduct a post-emergency After-Action Review within 30 calendar days of the resolution of any declared emergency, documenting: what worked, what failed, patient adverse events attributable to the emergency, and corrective actions to improve the emergency response for future events. After-Action Review findings shall be reported to the QAPI Committee and to the Governing Body.

5. Definitions

6. Procedures

6.1 Patient Emergency Risk Stratification — Ongoing Maintenance

6.2 Technology-Dependent Patient Emergency Planning

6.3 Emergency Preparedness Care Plan — Development and Maintenance

6.4 Clinical Emergency Response Protocol — Activation and Execution

6.5 Post-Emergency Restoration of Normal Services

6.6 Escalation and Exception Handling

7. Documentation Requirements

8. Compliance & Audit Considerations

8.1 How Compliance Is Measured

8.2 What Surveyors and Auditors Will Look For

CMS surveyors conducting an emergency preparedness survey under the Emergency Preparedness Rule (42 CFR § 484.102) will specifically verify:

Evidence of a risk-based Emergency Operations Plan. The plan must be based on an all-hazards risk assessment for the agency's geographic area. Surveyors will request the EOP and verify it is current, approved by the Governing Body, and tested annually.

Evidence of patient-level emergency planning. Surveyors will select a sample of active patient clinical records and verify that emergency risk categories are documented and that Category 1 and 2 patients have completed EPCPs. The absence of patient-level emergency planning is a frequently cited deficiency.

Evidence that technology-dependent patients have device-specific contingency plans. Surveyors will select patients with powered medical equipment and verify that the EPCP addresses device failure scenarios and that utility company registration has been completed.

Evidence of emergency communications planning. Surveyors will verify that the agency has a communication plan that addresses how patients will be contacted during emergencies and how clinical staff will receive emergency direction.

Evidence of annual training and testing. Surveyors will request training completion records and After-Action Reviews from any emergency events that occurred in the prior year.

Evidence of coordination with community partners. Surveyors will look for documented relationships with the local Healthcare Coalition, county emergency management, and utility providers.

8.3 Common Failure Points

9. References

9.1 Federal Regulations

9.2 CMS Guidance

9.3 Cross-Referenced Agency Policies

10. Training & Acknowledgment Requirements

10.1 All clinical staff shall receive initial training on clinical emergency preparedness within 14 calendar days of hire and annually thereafter, covering: the Patient Emergency Risk Stratification System and category criteria; the EPCP development process; technology-dependent patient contingency planning; the Clinical Emergency Response Protocol and priority contact timelines; emergency contact documentation requirements; and the After-Action Review process.

10.2 All on-call and after-hours nursing staff shall receive specific training on emergency activation procedures, Category 1 patient welfare check protocols, and emergency-period clinical triage decision-making.

10.3 Clinical staff shall participate in the agency's annual emergency preparedness drill per RM-EP-002. Clinical participation in the drill shall be documented in each clinician's training record.

10.4 All personnel within scope shall sign the Policy Acknowledgment Form (Appendix E) within 14 calendar days of the effective date, any revision, or hire/appointment.

10.5 The Director of Nursing shall maintain training completion records for all clinical staff and shall report any non-compliance to the Administrator within 7 calendar days of any training deadline.

11. Version Control

11.1 This policy is maintained under the agency's enterprise policy lifecycle management system per policy EN-LC-001.

11.2 Only the most current approved version of this policy, as reflected in the policy header, is valid for any operational, compliance, or regulatory purpose. All superseded versions must be archived and clearly marked as "SUPERSEDED — NOT FOR USE."

11.3 Any substantive revision to this policy requires: (a) review and approval by the Governing Body, documented in meeting minutes; (b) re-acknowledgment by all personnel within scope within 14 calendar days of the revised effective date; (c) update to the enterprise policy index per EN-TG-001.

11.4 Non-substantive revisions (formatting, typographical corrections, updated cross-references) may be approved by the Administrator with notification to the Governing Body at the next regular meeting.
